# MANUAL
## OF
# XÉFOLIUS. <note>Xéfolius is a pseudonym widely attributed to the Marquis de Chefdebien (1753–1814), a high-ranking French Freemason and member of the "Philalethes," an Enlightenment-era group dedicated to the study of the occult and alchemy.</note>

## AT THE <term>GRAND ORIENT</term>. <note>The "Grand Orient" refers to the Grand Orient de France, the main governing body of French Freemasonry. Books from this era often listed the "Grand Orient" as their place of publication to identify them as works intended for a Masonic audience.</note>

### 1788.

<note>original vocabulary: The Manual of Xéfolius, At the Grand Orient, 1788, freemasonry</note>

<summary>This is the title page for an 18th-century Masonic and philosophical treatise published under the pseudonym Xéfolius. Printed just before the French Revolution, the work promises universal wisdom to any reader, regardless of their background.</summary>
<keywords>Freemasonry, Xéfolius, Grand Orient, 18th century, occultism, Marquis de Chefdebien, Enlightenment</keywords>

--- Page 10 ---
<meta>This page serves as the opening "Editor's Notice" (Avis de l'Éditeur), a common literary device in the 18th century where the author poses as an editor who has "discovered" a manuscript to lend the story an air of authenticity.</meta>

# EDITOR’S NOTICE.

Even if I were to say by what means the work I am now giving to the public fell into my hands, the extraordinary nature of this story would satisfy the reader no more than my silence could trouble them, and it would add nothing to the inestimable value of the gift I am presenting. Surprised and preoccupied by this singularity, I read with a kind of mistrust; but soon my conjectures were stifled by admiration: I found what no philosopher had yet offered us, a complete system <term>systême complet: a comprehensive philosophical framework meant to explain the totality of existence</term>. I felt my mind lean upon and settle on a foundation that corresponded to it in every way; I felt my soul rise and expand; I felt my heart catch fire with a new love for my fellow beings; my imagination was struck with a deeper respect for the Author of all things <note>original: "l'Auteur de toutes choses" — a common Enlightenment-era reference to God or a Deist Creator</note>; I saw the "why" behind so many subjects of complaint against eternal wisdom; finally, finding myself better and happier <note>The use of feminine adjectives in the original French—"meilleure" and "plus heureuse"—indicates that the "Editor" persona is a woman</note>, I thought that it was not by chance that I had been chosen, and that Providence <term>Providence: the protective care of God or nature as a spiritual power</term> had determined me to be the instrument of its publication

<summary>In this preface, a female editor explains how she came to publish a manuscript that she believes offers a profound and life-changing philosophical system. She describes the work as a gift to the reader that justifies the mysteries of divine wisdom and inspires a deeper love for humanity.</summary>
<keywords>editor's preface, philosophy, Providence, Enlightenment, moral improvement, divine wisdom</keywords>

--- Page 11 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Editor's Notice" from page iii. The sentence begins mid-thought, following the editor’s statement that she was chosen by Providence to be the instrument of the "publication" of this manual.</meta>

# EDITOR’S NOTICE.

of this manual, suitable for all faiths <term>cultes: refers to organized religions or systems of worship</term>, which it respects; for all ages, which it instructs; for all walks of life, which it consoles—from the monarch to the beggar. Sentiment and reason <note>original: "Le sentiment & la raison" — the author invokes the two pillars of 18th-century thought to justify her decision</note> have led me, in agreement, to share with my brothers the encouraging hopes, the peaceful resignation, and the stirrings toward perfection with which I found myself filled. Strengthened by a happiness that was unknown to me until then, I brave the ridicule that "strong minds" <term>esprits forts: a term for freethinkers or skeptics who prioritized secular reason over spiritual or "mystical" experiences</term>—who are actually weak—will cast upon me; and in advance, I forgive them for the sorrows they may wish to inflict in exchange for the happiness to which I invite the reader, and which sooner or later will become their own.

<meta>This page marks the beginning of the main text, titled "The Manual of Xéfolius," following the Editor's Notice. The editor has finished her introductory remarks and now presents the first book of the manuscript.</meta>

# THE MANUAL
### OF
## XÉFOLIUS.

### BOOK ONE.

->I.<-

I asked myself one day: By what path did you arrive at thinking as you think? What are the events, the pleasures, the pains that have MODIFIED YOUR SENTIMENT <note>original: "sentiment" — in the 18th-century context, this refers to a combination of moral intuition and internal conviction, rather than just emotion</note> & determined your opinion? Then I regretted not having kept an exact journal of my principal observations and the consequences that flowed from them, because I would see today through how many ERRORS MAN MUST PASS TO ARRIVE AT THE TRUTH which he is compelled to seek until he has found it.

This collection of my thoughts would perhaps prevail over all the books of the philosophers <term>philosophes: the intellectual figures of the Enlightenment who applied reason to study society, nature, and the soul</term> that I have ever read, and in which one finds at every

<summary>The author opens the first book of the manual with a self-reflective inquiry into how their personal experiences and trials have shaped their current beliefs. They argue that the journey toward truth is inevitably paved with errors and suggest that an honest record of this process is more valuable than formal philosophy.</summary>
<keywords>introspection, intellectual development, search for truth, enlightenment philosophy, personal journal, nature of error</keywords>

--- Page 13 ---
<meta>The previous page ended with the author critiquing traditional philosophy books, noting that one finds "at every [page]" certain buzzwords without clear definitions. This page continues that critique by naming specific schools of thought and historical figures.</meta>

<header>THE MANUAL</header>

page the grand words *wisdom*, *virtue*, *sovereign good* <term>sovereign good: the "summum bonum," or the highest good that serves as the ultimate goal of human life</term>, &c., without learning there what wisdom, virtue, or the sovereign good actually are. The gods of Epicurus <note>Epicurus (341–270 BC) taught that the gods exist but do not involve themselves in human affairs</note> are but lazy mortals, indifferent to the fate of men, who, according to him, are also nothing more than machines organized by chance. Pyrrho <note>founder of the school of Skepticism</note> doubts everything, even his own existence; the apathetic Stoic claims that Aristides <note>Aristides "The Just," an Athenian statesman known for his integrity</note> was as unjust as Phalaris <note>a Sicilian tyrant famous for his cruelty, specifically the "brazen bull" execution device</note>; Brasidas <note>a renowned Spartan general</note> as cowardly as Dolon <note>a Trojan spy in the Iliad depicted as a coward</note>; Plato as ungrateful as Meletus <note>the primary accuser in the trial of Socrates</note>. By placing the love of glory among the virtues, Themistocles is praised because the victories of Miltiades <note>Themistocles' rival and the hero of the Battle of Marathon</note> kept him from sleeping, without considering that, to be consistent, one would also have to praise him for having the "Just Man" of Greece exiled, since it was equally the love of glory that drove him to envy and persecute Aristides; a love which differs in no way from the love of other objects that men cannot possess in equal measure, and whose value varies with climates, governments, and the centuries. Everywhere, the love of glory is nothing but the love of status, of praise, of power—the principle of discord <note>original: "difcordances" — an archaic spelling</note> hostile to rest, and the source of divisions and innumerable evils. If this

<summary>The author critiques various ancient philosophical schools—including Epicureans, Skeptics, and Stoics—for their perceived moral inconsistencies. He specifically attacks the "love of glory," arguing it is not a virtue but a destructive desire for status that breeds envy and social division.</summary>
<keywords>criticism of philosophy, Epicureanism, Stoicism, Pyrrhonism, Aristides the Just, Themistocles, love of glory, moral philosophy, Enlightenment thought</keywords>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>The previous page concluded a critique of ancient philosophers and historical figures, arguing that their definitions of virtue and glory were inconsistent and failed to provide a clear foundation for the "sovereign good."</meta>

-># ON XÉFOLIÜS. #<-

supreme ambition produces a real good only when the gathering of circumstances is such that its own interest is linked to the general interest. What then can I learn from these philosophers? Where is the foundation of what they claim to teach? In what consists this sovereign good <term>sovereign good: the "summum bonum," the ultimate goal of human life</term> which they promise?

However, just as having failed to do something useful in one's time is no reason not to do something else that may be no less useful, I am going to work from memory and compose for myself a manual of **HARMONIOUS CONSEQUENCES**.

But as it is important for my happiness to leave this world without any of my brothers having cause to complain about me, I shall write it in such a way that it cannot, in any case, compromise anyone.

If by chance this writing should one day fall into the hands of the public, every author shall be free to reclaim whatever they find in it belonging to them, which I can no longer restore to them—for the good things I have read have become my very flesh and blood. Nevertheless, far from wishing to appropriate the property of others, I willingly offer my own to whoever shall desire to make use of it for their—

# THE MANUAL #

profit, because the just and good God COULD NOT HAVE PLACED HAPPINESS IN OBJECTS THAT ALL MEN COULD NOT EQUALLY ATTAIN, and that which is indifferent to this happiness—which ALL MEN ARE COMPELLED TO SEEK, and which is the DRIVING FORCE OF ALL THEIR DESIRES, OF ALL THEIR MOTIONS, AND OF EVERYTHING THAT AGITATES THEM—does not deserve even a single glance.

If, when I expand upon the causes of OUR SUCCESSIVE DEVELOPMENT; if, when laying down the HARMONIOUS PRINCIPLES, according to which I establish a system conforming to the nature of our being—such as a confidant of the Eternal <note>The "Eternal" is a common Enlightenment-era term for God, emphasizing His timeless nature.</note> might do—I happen to speak of them like one inspired, and address my words to men; I can give no other reason for it, except that I feel forced to do so: for far from wishing to be believed on my word alone, I warn that man should only yield to the internal assent of the HARMONIOUS point; <note>The "harmonious point" likely refers to a central state of internal balance or conscience that recognizes truth.</note> and that provided he is covered and does not fall, the various ways in which different peoples or different individuals of the same people clothe themselves, support themselves, act...

<vocab>God, happiness, men, development, harmonious principles, the Eternal, internal feeling, Manual</vocab>

<summary>The author argues that God has made true happiness accessible to everyone and explains that his writing is driven by an internal necessity rather than a desire for blind authority. He encourages readers to judge his "harmonious principles" by their own internal conscience.</summary>
<keywords>divine justice, universal happiness, human development, harmonious principles, internal assent, Enlightenment theology</keywords>

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's argument from page 2, concluding the thought that external differences in customs or beliefs are secondary to internal truth.</meta>

->FROM XÉFOLIUS.<-
A 3

...all innocent, so long as they do not wound the ROOTS OF IMMUTABLE TRUTHS, from which we cannot depart without stumbling, without wandering into darkness—in a word, without suffering.

COPERNICUS & TYCHO BRAHE <note>Tycho Brahe (1546–1601) was a Danish astronomer whose observations provided the basis for Kepler's laws of planetary motion.</note> have overthrown the system of Ptolemy <note>Ptolemy’s geocentric model, placing Earth at the center of the universe, was the standard astronomical manual for over 1,500 years.</note>, which had reigned for sixteen centuries; and if that of Copernicus has prevailed, it is certainly not because it is self-evident, but only because it is the most plausible, and because it yields more satisfying results than the others.

Thus, if what I offer you, in order to support your curious and wavering spirit, is not to your taste, you may choose a system that pleases you more, without fear of harming your happiness or your salvation; these depend on the foundation of truths for which you have a feeling, and which I reveal to your mind, but not on arbitrary forms, or those secrets that the human mind cannot conceive and even less penetrate. Let this be said once and for all.

## II.

Learned men have not yet been able to agree on the height of certain mountains a hundred times...

<summary>The author argues that while scientific systems like those of Copernicus or Ptolemy may change based on probability, spiritual happiness rests on "immutable truths" felt within the soul. He grants the reader the freedom to choose their own conceptual system, provided they remain anchored to these core moral foundations.</summary>

<keywords>Copernicus, Tycho Brahe, Ptolemy, immutable truth, scientific systems, religious salvation, spiritual feeling, astronomical history</keywords>

THE MANUAL

measured according to infallible rules; and yet they dare to put the Great Architect <note>A common Enlightenment-era term for God, framing the Creator as a master builder or cosmic designer.</note> in the hot seat, to make Him account for the disorders they claim to discover in nature; as if the requirements of an infinite work and of **UNIVERSAL HARMONY** could be the same as those of the presumptuous man, the sybarite <note>A person devoted to luxury and pleasure, named after the ancient Greek city of Sybaris.</note>, the egoist, the proud man, etc.! They know well enough what grass needs to make it grow; but do they know why a blade of grass organizes and develops in one way rather than another? Yet they seek to guess how an immense whole was organized and developed, of which—even with a hundred million years of life and calculations—they would still know next to nothing. Conceiving the formation of the universe is to the strength of the human mind what the weight of that same universe is to the strength of his body.

Knowing, therefore, that the knowledge we can acquire is limited to the discovery of very few of nature’s works and laws; certain, nonetheless, that the need to know was not given to me in vain, **BECAUSE NATURE HAS DONE NOTHING IN VAIN**, I

will not merely occupy myself with seeking by what means certain lands were populated; first, because besides the fact that this knowledge is not necessary for our happiness, it is also that with all possible instruction and sagacity, man, in this kind of research, can never offer anything but conjectures that often destroy themselves; second, because I am no more surprised that humans were found in America than wolves, deer, dogs, bears, elk, squirrels, rabbits, etc. <note>The question of how humans and animals reached the Americas was a major debate in the 18th century, often pitting biblical accounts of Noah's Ark against emerging scientific observations.</note>: but what would surprise me greatly would be if this half of the globe had been found deserted until the day when descendants *of the seven of each species* <note>original: "des sept de chaque espèce"—a reference to Genesis 7:2, where Noah is commanded to take seven pairs of "clean" animals into the Ark.</note>, saved from the deluge in Noah’s ark, as intrepid navigators, had gone to settle there, each with his mate. Therefore, to respond to the promptings I feel, I shall concern myself only with the <term>science of man</term> <note>In the Enlightenment, the "science of man" referred to the multidisciplinary study of human nature, encompassing what we now call psychology, sociology, and ethics.</note>.

# THE MANUAL

measured according to infallible rules; and yet they dare to place the **Great Architect** <note>A term frequently used in Enlightenment Deism and Freemasonry to describe God as a rational creator and master builder of a clockwork universe.</note> on the witness stand, to make him account for the disorders they claim to discover in nature; as if the requirements of an infinite work and of **UNIVERSAL HARMONY** could be the same as those of the presumptuous man, the *sybarite* <note>A person who is self-indulgent or devoted to sensuous luxury.</note>, the egotist, the proud man, etc.! They know well what grass needs to make it grow; but do they know why a blade of grass organizes and develops in one way rather than another? Nevertheless, they seek to guess how an immense whole was organized and developed, of which, even with a hundred million years of life and calculations, they would still know almost nothing. To conceive the formation of the universe is to the strength of the human mind what the weight of that same universe is to the strength of his body.

Knowing then that the knowledge we can acquire is limited to the discovery of very few works and laws of nature; sure, nonetheless, that the need to know was not given to me in vain, **BECAUSE NATURE HAS DONE NOTHING IN VAIN** <note>original: "PARCE QUE LA NATURE N'A RIEN FAIT EN VAIN"—a reference to the Aristotelian axiom *Natura nihil facit frustra*, common in 18th-century philosophy to argue for a purposeful design in the world.</note>, I

--- Page 20 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's philosophical inquiry. On the previous page, he critiqued the arrogance of those who try to judge the "Universal Harmony" of the universe without understanding its laws. # OF XÉFOLIUS.

I will not occupy myself solely by seeking the means by which certain lands were populated; first, because beyond the fact that such knowledge is not necessary for our happiness, it is also because, with all possible education and wisdom, man, in this type of research, can never offer anything but conjectures that often contradict themselves. Secondly, I am no more surprised to have found men in **America** than to have found wolves, stags, dogs, bears, moose, squirrels, rabbits, etc.; but what would surprise me greatly would be if this half of the globe had remained deserted until the day when the descendants *of the seven of each species* <note>A reference to Genesis 7:2, where Noah is commanded to take seven pairs of every "clean" animal onto the ark to preserve their lineage.</note>, saved from the **flood** in **Noah’s ark**—those intrepid navigators—had gone to settle there, each with his mate. Therefore, to respond to the impulses I feel, I shall concern myself only with the **science of man** <note>During the Enlightenment, the "science of man" (*science de l'homme*) referred to the study of human nature, ethics, and society through observation and reason, rather than through religious dogma.</note>.

### III.

After having considered the works of **nature** that were within my reach, and having found in each of them so many wonders, I raised my soul toward the **Master of all things**, and

<header>THE MANUAL</header>

I said to Him: "O my God! Where shall I find the truth that it is vital for me to know?" And it was answered to me:

> "The Eternal One <note>original: *L'Éternel*. A term for God frequently used in French philosophical and mystical texts of the 18th century to emphasize His timelessness.</note> has impressed laws upon all His works; none reaches its destiny except by the virtue of these laws. Study your heart, because it is in your heart that He has impressed the laws of the nature of your destined being—laws by the virtue of which you must arrive at happiness and work out your salvation. For, as it is not the most learned, but the best learned <note>The author contrasts the quantity of knowledge (*plus savant*) with the quality and moral application of wisdom (*mieux savant*).</note> who is the most happy, all sciences are in vain for those who do not possess the **science of man**; and whoever has the science of man can do without all others. This science requires no study on the part of those who live by feeling, within the laws of their destined nature; but it requires a profound and well-directed study from those who have strayed from them.
> Begin, then, by considering what nature of happiness belongs to those who awe you with their rank or their intellect; see all that can disturb this happiness, seek out what its needs are, and finally weigh what its consistency might be."

<vocab>The Eternal One, laws, heart, destiny, happiness, salvation, science of man</vocab>

<summary>The author describes a divine revelation directing him away from external academic study and toward the "science of man" found within the human heart. It suggests that true happiness comes from following the internal laws of one's nature rather than seeking worldly rank or intellect.</summary>

<keywords>inner truth, divine laws, heart, science of man, spiritual happiness, salvation, Enlightenment philosophy</keywords>

### ON XEFOLIUS.

> "The peoples who exchange their gold for glass beads <note>The author uses a common 18th-century metaphor for colonial trade to illustrate a foolish exchange of values.</note> give away a thing that is useless to them, for another that they could just as easily do without. But when man thinks to turn the social virtues of his brothers to his own profit, by violating the laws of his being, what is he? what does he deserve? what kind of trade is he making?"

->IV.<-

WHAT has become of so many heroes whose names history has not passed down to us? What were the names of the kings who reigned between Ninias and Sardanapalus? <note>Ninias and Sardanapalus were legendary kings of Assyria; the author refers to the vast, "lost" periods of ancient history to show the futility of worldly fame.</note> who built the pyramids of Egypt? how many Hercules <note>original: *Hercules*. Ancient scholars often argued there were several distinct figures who contributed to the myths of Hercules.</note> have there been? Were all these gods, all these demi-gods of fable, extraordinary men, real beings, or allegories imagined by the poets? What difference is there today between the conqueror of India <note>Likely a reference to Alexander the Great or the mythical conquests of Bacchus.</note> and the obscure and peaceful citizen who disappeared from the earth at the same time? Does it matter to the one that it is forgotten that he lived, and to the other that people still speak of him? Which was the happier? which is more so now? What is happiness? Does it reside in some situation where the caprice of a powerful man

<meta>This page continues the speaker's inquiry into the nature of true happiness. Following the previous page's discussion on the futility of worldly fame and the whims of the powerful, the text now shifts to a philosophical contrast between the physical body and the immortal soul.</meta>

THE MANUAL

disposed, or is it rather a state of the SOUL?

Matter gravitates toward its origin, seeking rest; but the spirit rises, leaping into the infinite, and everything that confines it causes it pain. I see a physical world, and I feel a moral world: but I observe that the more man enriches himself with perishable objects, and the more he extends his fleeting existence, the more he starves himself with needs. What then is this law of our being which drives us toward the vastness of the infinite, and which nothing here below can satisfy?

### V.

**I HAVE** seen youth burning with desires; I have seen middle age devoured by ambition; I have seen infirm old age, still cradling itself with fanciful projects, drawing its last breath with its eyes fixed upon a rattle <note>The "rattle" (*hochet*) is a recurring Enlightenment metaphor for the trivialities, honors, and titles that adults pursue, comparing them to infants distracted by toys.</note> from which it hoped for happiness; while the innocent lamb, following laws it cannot violate, lives in profound peace.

I have studied the great ones of the earth; they are bursting with possessions, sorrows, desires, ambition, and boredom: they are like dropsical men <note>original: *hydropiques*. Dropsy (edema) was a common medical metaphor in the 18th century for greed; just as the ailment makes one swell with fluid yet remain thirsty, the ambitious man acquires more yet remains unsatisfied.</note> who die of thirst.

I heard the sybarite <note>A person devoted to luxury and pleasure, named after the ancient Greek city of Sybaris, known for its decadence.</note> calling for happiness—

### VI.

I PERCEIVED that all those with whom I lived were distracting themselves <note>The original French "s’étourdissoient" implies a frantic state of numbing one's mind to avoid facing reality.</note> and groaning; I understood that they expected happiness from some future event; but that, no matter what occurred, happiness never arrived, because no one thought to study the nature of their being, and the nature of the good we are compelled to seek. And after having likewise lived both in the palaces of kings and in the cottage of the poor, I have returned, fully persuaded that happiness neither flees nor inhabits any region exclusively; but that, wishing to be happy while clinging to error and injustice, persisting in becoming so through the very obstacle that opposes it, and finding only uncertainties, cares, and agitations in its proud misery, **THE MISGUIDED MAN** <term>Misguided man: From the French "l’homme dévoyé," describing one who has strayed from the natural path of truth and virtue.</term> must either deny or blaspheme Providence <note>The protective care of God or nature as a spiritual power.</note>, the consoler of the simple man who adores it; and that, from the throne to the cabin, one must necessarily hear it said that there is no happiness for us on earth.

## VII.

What would become of society if nature had not given hands to man? Without offensive or defensive weapons, without the means to forge them, he would not have been long in becoming the prey of other animals. But every craftsman has a model, a design, and a goal. He who gave hands to man, gave to each of the different species the instruments and intelligence necessary for their needs, as well as the needs and inclinations necessary for the **HARMONY OF THE GREAT WHOLE** <term>Harmony of the Great Whole: A philosophical concept suggesting that every element in nature is perfectly balanced and serves a specific purpose within a divine or natural system.</term>. With what wisdom and foresight he has provided for their clothing, their food, and their shelter!

But when a thoughtless person <note>original: "l'étourdi." ->VIII.<-

**WHY** did the carnivorous animals <note>original: "animaux carnassiers."</note> not league themselves against man before he had forged weapons? Why do the lion, the bear, the tiger, and the wolf not form a confederation to defend themselves against the hunters who pursue them? Why does man subdue all the animals that can be useful to him? Why does a herd of oxen allow itself to be put under the yoke by a child without a whip? Why does the reindeer not live alongside the elephant, and why does each species have its own climate, while man lives and reigns in all climates? Why? It is because every craftsman has a model, a design, and a goal, and because the **Eternal** <note>original: "l'Eternel." A common Enlightenment-era reference to God or the Creator, emphasizing a rational, law-giving deity.</note> has imprinted on each of his works laws suited to making them all contribute to the **HARMONY OF THE GREAT WHOLE**, and to the final **DESTINY OF EACH OF HIS WORKS**.

### IX.

**WHY** do no animals of the same species wage war on one another as men do—men, whom the restlessness of an insatiable need <note>original: "besoin insatiable." In this context, it refers to a psychological or spiritual craving rather than just physical hunger.</note> drives from one pole to the other to destroy one another and seek the seeds of new wars and a multitude of new evils? Why does the satiated lion wish harm on no one else? And why is it only after having satisfied his physical needs that man experiences most intensely that **moral need**, which often leads him to a terrifying ferocity? Because you have eyes, you see; you walk because you have feet; you eat because you are hungry; and you rule over other animals because you have hands. This is the entire substance of this philosophy, to which false sages and the common people give a morose <note>The original French *hypochondriaque* in the 18th century referred to a state of deep melancholy or "spleen," suggesting that critics viewed this philosophy as joyless or overly serious.</note> face, and which they depict as an enemy of pleasures, while in truth it permits them all; it forbids only excesses and offenses against the various harmonies. Our transitory nature has laws of necessity, conforming to the nature of our being, to its essence, and to its end; and our destined nature <note>original: *nature de destinée*; this refers to the spiritual or eternal aspect of humanity that survives after death, contrasted with the physical "transitory" body.</note> has necessary laws conforming to its essence and its end. The first of these two natures is drawn toward the earth where it must return; the second is drawn toward the immensity which will be its portion. One, being mortal, tends toward rest; the other, being immortal, tends toward the infinite. # CONCERNING XÉFOLIUS.

B 4

clearly imprinted with social laws. Let us now see what becomes of, and what must be experienced by, this social being, when he misuses his liberty to violate or displace the laws that have been imprinted upon him, and whose sentiment he cannot stifle.

Suppose a gathering of disciples of the doctrine which teaches that there is no God; that our soul is mortal; that there are no other pleasures than physical pleasures; and that the more power and wealth we possess, the happier we shall be, because wealth and power are the means by which we can surround ourselves with slaves occupied with warding off pain from us and procuring us physical pleasures.

We see first that these proponents of the <term>doctrine of tigers</term> <note>original: "doctrine des tigres." A metaphorical term used by the author to describe a predatory philosophy of extreme self-interest and lack of moral restraint.</note> would infallibly be worse than the beasts themselves; for if such were the nature of man, we would never have gathered together in society, and it would then be quite impossible for us to know both these terms and those tender and harmonious sentiments, the slightest touch of which moves us. We see further that this doctrine legitimizes both ambition and avarice, and all the other crimes which [it]...

->->THE MANUAL<-<-

What strange ideas man can form of happiness when he falls into depravity! Yet, fortunately, the laws of our being and universal laws act upon him without ceasing; and even one who systematically opposes them is forced a hundred times over to pay them homage—whether unknowingly, or by yielding to their promptings, or by succumbing to the painful retribution they exact within him.

### XIV.

Once man—so restricted in his capacity to know, yet so vast in his desires—sends his intellect forth alone to discover what is most useful to believe or to perform, he finds no place to rest, and even the most obvious truths become questionable to him. # DE XÉFOLIUS.

the same for the consequence she <note>Refers back to the "School of the Porch" or the personified philosophy mentioned previously.</note> drew from it—a consequence contradicted by the feelings of all men—when she said: *He who possesses the highest good cannot be unhappy; thus the sage is as happy in the bull of Phalaris as at the table with his friends.* <note>original: "le taureau de Phalaris." Phalaris was a notorious tyrant of ancient Sicily who allegedly roasted his enemies alive inside a hollow bronze bull. Stoic philosophers used this extreme example to argue that internal virtue is so powerful that it allows a person to remain "happy" even while undergoing the most horrific physical torture.</note> Certainly, if there existed a man inaccessible to pain, he would find this consequence perfectly just. But since we are all of the same species; since we have all received the same physical and moral laws; since He who gave us our **intellect** <term>esprit</term> also gave us our **feeling** <term>sentiment</term>; and since, in giving us intellect and feeling as guides, He did not give them to us together so that we might consult only one of the two; we must never separate them when reasoning. For, wherever they are in agreement, an immutable truth is found; and whatever is diametrically opposed to this agreement is an obvious error. It is from this harmony that the faculty we call **judgment** proceeds, which is lacking in anyone who has formed the habit of not reuniting them. It is to this agreement that it pleased the Creator to limit our necessary knowledge; and finally, be-

->### THE MANUAL<-

beyond this limit, we encounter nothing but uncertainty, trouble & confusion; & such is the agreement that I call **THE HARMONIOUS POINT.**

When the great principle reveals to some worshiper of wisdom a corner of the veil that hides the past & the future, it is not to satisfy a vain curiosity, but to pour joy into this pious heart, and to advance it in its modification. <note>In the context of 18th-century spiritual philosophy, "modification" refers to the internal transformation or refinement of the soul’s state.</note> What this worshiper of eternal wisdom will then believe he can communicate to men will doubtless surpass their intelligence, but will in no way clash with **THE HARMONIOUS POINT**, or the **MORAL PRISM** through which they must examine everything that can influence their happiness & their salvation.

Always seek, therefore, the harmonious point of everything that man must believe or do: for example, we all have a feeling for the beauty of justice, and reason tells us that where justice does not reign, disorder reigns; & I shall say the same of pity, of love for our fellow men, etc.

Anger & hatred are painful to the feeling, and reason shows us their dangers.

<meta>This page continues the critique of the Greek philosopher Epicurus. ### THE MANUAL

All the genius of this great philosopher did not protect him from the obvious folly of seeking to conceive of an infinite work, of which one would need to know every single constituent part to arrive at a knowledge of all the relationships they have with one another... And what a distance still between the mechanism and the mechanic! <note>In this context, the "mechanism" refers to the physical universe, while the "mechanic" refers to a divine creator or primary mover—a distinction the author believes Epicurus failed to bridge.</note>

Unable, therefore, to explain either the formation of the universe or the principle that dictated its laws, Epicurus set about composing a system where the soul is mortal; but in seeking happiness, he was forced to return to the **harmonious point** <note>The author’s central concept: a state of balance where the heart and mind are in accord.</note>.

There, armed with his prism, he saw that social happiness is the result of social harmony; that what troubled society had begun—and continued—to trouble the individual, and he concluded that individual happiness is likewise the result of individual harmony.

Passing from there to the needs of man, and to the pleasures he procures for himself by satisfying those needs, as well as the pains he brings upon himself by overstepping them, Epicurus taught that since the *more* must prevail over the *less*, one had to accept a lesser pain to obtain a greater pleasure, and sacrifice a great

<meta>This page continues the critique of Epicureanism from page 34. The previous page ended mid-sentence, arguing that Epicurus failed to find true harmony because he focused only on physical sensation rather than the moral and spiritual laws of human nature.</meta>

# THE MANUAL

...in the long intervals necessary for the rebirth of these needs, that the moral being, urged toward happiness, is tormented by the displacement of its laws; could the Epicurean—who believed only in gods as idle and indifferent as himself, who circumscribed his law of love, and who expected nothing but nothingness <note>original: *néant*. In this context, it refers to the Epicurean belief that the soul does not survive death.</note> as his end—find anything other than a dull, restless peace within dispositions so ill-suited to the nature of his being? Could he know the full sweetness of a harmonious existence, and not frequently miss that supreme good which we demand in vain from objects? For this good is the effect of the union of the nature of things relative to the nature of our being, and the result of harmony.

Nevertheless, the Epicurean was infinitely happier than those men of the whirlwind <note>original: *tourbillon*. This likely refers to the frantic, chaotic pace of "polite society" or those caught up in the mindless pursuit of fashion and social status.</note> who, living by chance and not even knowing they possess a soul, believe they can appease the promptings of their destined nature by over-stimulating and exhausting the springs of their transient nature, until the laws of that nature awaken only to punish them. If I turn to the depraved of another kind, what will they teach me?

### OF XEFOLIUS.

...another type <term>type: in this philosophical system, a "type" refers to the physical blueprint or species-form of a living creature.</term>, there is, I say, none that is not developed by a particle of the same essence of which our moral being is composed.

I ask whether, with all his genius, Buffon <note>Georges-Louis Leclerc, Comte de Buffon (1707–1788), was the most famous French naturalist of the Enlightenment. The author uses him here as the ultimate example of human intellect.</note> himself would know how to spin his web, if he found himself suddenly enclosed within the type of a spider? By what would he be distinguished? How would he make it known that this type contains a great man? Without the laws of the spider, he would be the clumsiest and most unhappy of these animals. But other laws, other thoughts, other effects; and as evidence would be equivalent to violence <note>The author suggests that if a soul had full "evidence" (conscious memory) of its higher nature while trapped in a lower animal form, the discrepancy would be a form of psychological torture or "violence."</note>, we should find the memory of the past only in the realms of harmony.

Since the seeds <term>seeds: *germes*, referring to the fundamental units of life or potential beings scattered throughout nature, a common concept in 18th-century vitalism.</term> spread throughout nature can never fail, the successive transmigration of a being could never be halted, because, by its essence, it possesses a speed that even that of electric fire <note>original: *feu électrique*. In the 18th century, electricity was often described as a "fire" or "fluid" and was considered the fastest and most subtle force in the universe.</note> could not approach; and being destined to complete its labors in the particular ray upon which it began, when its work suffers a major accident, it leaves it to begin a similar one once more.

But all the rays of its circle lead...

### X I X.

**THE SUPREME BEING** being goodness by essence, how could he not have been necessitated to create beings to participate in his bliss? What would he have done with his goodness? # CONCERNING XEFOLIUS.

omnipotence, the goodness, and the justice of the Eternal?

-> XX. <-

**THE** God, being perfectly good, is also the perfectly just God. The Eternal, in creating you, may therefore have performed only an act of goodness. Thus, now that you exist, His justice owes you and will give you happiness; but to obtain this happiness, you must merit it, because God, being as good as He is just, cannot perform an act of goodness that is not also an act of justice, nor an act of justice that is not also an act of goodness. Therefore, so that you might merit the happiness for which you were created, He has made you free, and has imprinted upon you laws that you cannot fail to recognize, since you can neither displace nor violate them without immediately suffering for it, and without being warned and punished by the consequences of your infractions. As soon as you are in accord <note>Meaning: in alignment or agreement with divine law.</note>, as you feel you ought to be, you will ascend to bliss through the power <note>original: "vertu"</note> of the laws that the Eternal has imprinted upon you; but as long as you are not harmonious, you will return to the crucible <term>crucible: A vessel used for melting and purifying metals; here, it is a metaphor for a state of spiritual refinement or reincarnation where the soul is "re-melted" to remove impurities.</term>; for, if you do not harmonize here, how would you harmonize there?

I do not come, armed with the sword, to demand the sacrifice of your reason nor of the feeling of

<sig>I i</sig>

# BOOK II.

## I.

I shall observe in this book neither sequence nor connection between the various thoughts that will compose it, for I have too many subjects to address, each of which would require several volumes to be fully illuminated. It will therefore be, strictly speaking, nothing more than a collection of religious and philosophical thoughts, of precepts, aphorisms <note>original: "sentences"; refers to short, pithy statements of moral truth.</note>, maxims, and reflections, presented as circumstances, events, and my memory provide them to me. Yet, as they all tend toward the same goal, and as I am concerned with only a single object, this collection will nonetheless form a body of enlightenment <note>original: "masse de lumières"; a quintessential Enlightenment-era metaphor suggesting that individual truths, when gathered, create a powerful radiance of reason.</note>. From this, great and consoling truths will flow, which will immediately strike those whose depth of experience is sufficient to follow my transitions and to supply the intermediate ideas that the boundaries I have set for this work have forced me to omit.

**Vocabulary:** thoughts, religious, philosophical, maxims, reflections, events, enlightenment.

<summary>The author introduces Book II as a non-linear collection of aphorisms and reflections, explaining that while the work lacks a formal structure, it remains unified by a single purpose: to provide a body of spiritual and philosophical enlightenment.</summary>
<keywords>Enlightenment, religious philosophy, maxims, aphorisms, intellectual intuition, book structure, philosophical methodology</keywords>

--- Page 66 ---
<meta>The text continues from the introduction of Book II, where the author established a non-linear, aphoristic structure. Here, he defends his use of repetition and begins discussing the relationship between sensation and the preservation of one’s essential form.</meta>

THE MANUAL OF XEFOLIUS. 51
<sig>D 2</sig>

If I sometimes seem to repeat myself, or to speak of something about which I have already spoken, as if I had not yet said anything on the matter, these apparent repetitions will occur only when I believe it necessary to further develop a truth, and to point out the sublime simplicity of the works of the **Great Architect** <note>original: "grand Architecte"; a term frequently used in Enlightenment-era philosophy and Freemasonry to describe a Creator who designed the universe according to rational, geometric laws.</note>. For example:

As we must modify ourselves through **sentiment** <note>original: "sentiment"; in this context, it refers to both physical sensation and the internal moral or emotional faculty of "feeling" truth.</note>, the same law which makes us susceptible to pleasures and pains—the law by which we are happy or unhappy—is also of primary necessity to that of the two natures which, in its movements, would break its type or its prototype, if the sensation of pain had not made its danger known. The types of plants, trees, etc., not running the same risk, are inaccessible to pain; and yet their organization and their development are no less admirable, and reach their end no less marvelously, by the virtue of the laws that form the instinct of the **passive agent**.

### III.

HE who knows nothing of **geometry** <term>geometry: in the 18th century, geometry was often used as a synonym for rigorous, logical reasoning and absolute certainty.</term>, is not at all surprised at being unable to solve a problem of **Euclid** <note>Euclid (c. 300 BCE) was the ancient Greek mathematician known as the "Father of Geometry."</note>; he will defer to the teacher's solution. But it is not so with the problems offered by human conduct; everyone imagines they can guess the principle, even though they do not possess the first datum of the nature of our being—which even **Pascal** <note>original: "Paschal"; referring to Blaise Pascal (1623–1662), the French mathematician and philosopher. The author critiques Pascal for abandoning rigorous mathematics for religious philosophy (Jansenism), which the author views as being "led astray."</note>, who had abandoned the study of geometry for that of man, only imperfectly knew, led astray as he was by opinions irreconcilable with the principle of all truth, and by his resistance to the assent of the harmonious point.

If, therefore, what I tell you seems obscure, you must blame your own ignorance and your prejudices; but read, reread my work, return to the movements of your heart; observe your fellow men, study the harmonious point, and you will not delay in recognizing the sun of truth within these apparent shadows.

<summary>The author argues that while people admit ignorance in mathematics, they wrongly assume they understand human nature. He critiques Blaise Pascal's philosophical turn and urges the reader to seek the "harmonious point" to find moral truth.</summary>

### IV.

THE Creator, having willed that man should reign over the earth and be modified within the state of society, has imprinted upon him laws that draw him closer to his fellow men, and has given him needs that force him to draw near to them. Man thus draws closer to his fellow men because he is attracted by laws of sentiment and by needs; but he has only received these laws of sentiment and these needs so that he might draw closer to his fellow men to be modified by them, and so that he might reach the end of his destiny.

Upon whichever of the laws of his being you choose to reflect, you will discover therein a principle of social destiny, and a principle of the future destiny of man. The blow he sees struck against a **sentient being** <term>sentient being: in the 18th century, this referred to any creature capable of feeling sensation or pain, emphasizing a biological and moral connection between humans and other living things.</term> wounds him; the fall he sees him take makes him shudder, stuns him: he feels his needs, he suffers from his wounds; why? because from this **sympathetic reaction** <note>original: "réaction sympathique"; refers to the concept of "sympathy" or "fellow-feeling," a central theme in Enlightenment ethics (notably in the works of Adam Smith or Rousseau).</note>, which we call **pity**, and whose modifying emotions give us cause for thought, emanates the command to spare and assist one another mutually; for if we ceased to assist one another, there would no longer be sociability, no longer society, and consequently, soon no more men; and that, by

# THE MANUAL

### V.

A CERTAIN bird, or a certain fly, on a journey of several hundred leagues, will not miss its longitude or latitude by a single minute; and men, with all their laborious sciences, never cease to lose their way every day; why? It is because this kind of **infallibility** <note>original: "infaillibilité"; here referring to the perfect precision of animal instinct as granted by nature.</note> is necessary to the fly and to the bird, and it is not at all so for man.

Let us count ourselves happy to have finally arrived at the gate of **felicity** <note>original: "félicité"; a state of supreme happiness or blessedness, often the goal of moral and philosophical systems of the era.</note>, but let us not take pride in the vain, uncertain, and dangerous knowledge that leads so many of our brothers astray.

Wretched as they are, driven by the need for individual harmony and flattering themselves with finding it in discordance, they argue against God and morality; they exhaust themselves in the search for a cohesive whole which they cannot manage to compose for themselves, chasing after the peace from which they distance themselves, and always suffering from their own existence.

What a way to enjoy a good, by beginning with the rejection of the first principle of that good! Is not the necessity of tracing back to a God in order to conceive of our own existence an evident proof of the existence of a God? And, given how necessary the laws of harmony are to any social system, could the happiness of the individual be independent of his obedience to these laws?

# THE MANUAL

*Lucius* yields to pity, to the love for his fellow men; he loves justice with passion. By listening only to his heart, *Lucius* would throw himself into the flames to rescue his father, his mother, his son, his friend. However, he imagines he believes that a son is the born enemy of his father; that the father reciprocates this feeling, and that he only loves his grandsons because he thinks he sees in them the enemies of his enemy. <note>This cynical view reflects a radical interpretation of self-interest, where family bonds are seen as mere strategic alliances against common rivals.</note>

*Lucius* further believes he believes that one can do nothing except out of self-interest; that this interest can be nothing other than a positive or negative pleasure, and that this pleasure, whether positive or negative, can only be physical. <note>Lucius represents the "materialist" or "sensalist" philosophers of the 18th century, like Helvetius, who argued that all human action is motivated solely by the pursuit of physical pleasure or the avoidance of pain.</note>

To prove all these fine discoveries, *Lucius* has written a book...; this book is only to be printed after his death...; death is, in his system, the annihilation of man...; and *Lucius* already tastes in advance the pleasure, which I believe to be very moral, of living in the memory of men. <note>The author points out a humorous irony: Lucius claims to believe death is total "annihilation," yet he finds joy in the idea of his reputation surviving him—a "moral" pleasure that his own theory of "purely physical" interest cannot explain.</note>

ONE who knows that where there is no air, there is no sound; that where there is no light, there is no color; that feeling is within us, and not in that which [affects] us <note>The text breaks off here as the author begins to explain that our perceptions (like sound or color) are internal experiences triggered by external forces, rather than properties existing independently in the objects themselves.</note>

->XV.<-

EVERYONE has their own particular object of affection from which they hope for happiness; and it is their power that must procure this object for them. One sees this power in their riches, another in their birth, a third in their beauty, a fourth in the superiority of their mind: but the child sees all these powers in physical strength; subjugated by it, they wish to be big and strong: an error that causes the child to delight in breaking everything they can reach, that makes youth turbulent, and mature age ambitious. The love of power is the folly of all men still in ignorance or disbelief regarding the purpose <note>original: "destination." In this philosophical context, it refers to the ultimate end, goal, or natural calling of a human being’s existence.</note> of their being, because it is through power that one procures external objects. Is it then surprising that what is most widely esteemed <note>original: "accrédité." Literally "accredited," meaning that which holds the most social capital or recognized influence.</note>, what is currently the most powerful, should be the most sought after; that everyone delights in exercising their strengths, whatever they may be; and would it not be surprising if truth, which unmasks all errors, were unanimously welcomed by men, when the only strong man is he who puts

### XVI.

It is just as common to see the great imagine they are of a superior nature to the lowly, as it is to see common people imagine that a "savage" <note>The author uses "sauvage" in the 18th-century sense to refer to indigenous peoples, specifically to critique the European assumption of superiority.</note> is a species of animal. Vanity, prejudice, and ignorance drive the thinking of both groups. AS we only carry over from worn-out types and prototypes the vague reminiscences of feeling, the less a man's feeling is modified by these reminiscences, the more experiences he will need to disabuse him of the objects from which the masses, despite the experience of so many centuries, hope for happiness. But the greatest truths only slide over man as long as he makes them nothing more than fodder for the mind and vanity, because he is guided more by impressions than by precepts, so long as his feelings are not sufficiently modified by painful sensations to penetrate their depth and utility. It is only as he is modified <note>The term "modified" here refers to a transformative process where the soul's character is reshaped by experience and suffering, making it more receptive to higher truths.</note> that the precepts corresponding to his experience are well received; that they untangle the chaos of his reminiscences <term>reminiscences: the idea, rooted in Platonic and mystical thought, that the soul carries a latent, clouded memory of its divine origin and original state.</term>, and that they enlighten him on the laws of his being from which they emanate.

# CONCERNING XÉFOLIUS <note>Xéfolius is a name often used in the mystical writings of Louis-Claude de Saint-Martin and his contemporaries to represent a specific philosophical perspective or a character in a spiritual dialogue.</note>

It is restless love or hatred, repugnance or desire, presumption or dejection, hope or despair, remorse, envy, terrors, anger, jealousy, fury, and vengeance that tear apart a heart made for peace and happiness; and this unfortunate man—jaded, bruised, infirm, and broken—dies at the very moment he hoped to begin living.

thought that since every need has a goal, the object of a need common to all men—a need that the possession of the entire globe could not satisfy—is an infinite need. Since the object of this infinite need is not found on earth, it must exist elsewhere. To doubt this would be as absurd as claiming that there exists only food that is either indigestible or devoid of substance; that we were given the need for nourishment only to hope and desire to appease a physical hunger, while, in truth, we were meant to remain forever starving.

No doubt this latter thought would never occur to anyone; but if there can be no doubt regarding our physical needs and the existence of the objects related to them, it is because our present existence is necessary for that destined existence <note>The author refers to the "existence de destinée," or the spiritual life that follows this earthly one, suggesting that our time on earth is a preparatory stage.</note> toward which we must strive and which we must deserve. ->XVIII.<-

There are men of genius who live in accordance with several of their laws, and who, though materialists in spirit, burn with a love for immortality and always see posterity before them: these men, who nurture their law of the infinite <note>The "Law of the Infinite" refers to the inherent human drive toward eternal or divine truths, a common concept in theosophical writing of this period.</note> outside of its true source <note>original: "orient." In this context, it refers to the spiritual dawn or the divine origin of the soul.</note>, are necessarily greatly troubled by it.

But should a man of genius, whose law of the infinite has its true source, dwell with pleasure on the thought that future generations might bless his memory, this secondary detail will not alter his happiness; and if this harmonious man is brought to the head of a nation, a moral awakening <note>original: "commotion morale." This implies a profound, soul-stirring movement within the citizenry.</note> will occur, capable of warming and restoring the senses of those most indifferent to the fate of the homeland.

<meta>This page continues the philosophical discourse on the "man of genius" and his role in society, specifically contrasting the expansive, immortal nature of true genius with the restrictive, destructive nature of the self-centered individual. # OF XÉFOLIUS <note>Xéfolius is a recurring character name in the works of the "Unknown Philosopher," Louis-Claude de Saint-Martin, often representing a seeker or a personified philosophical perspective.</note>

Just as there is no great idea that does not bear the imprint of immortality, so too is it only this sentiment that can inspire the man of genius, within the laws of his being, with the means most suited to create the happiness of the men he loves, and to maintain among them the harmony of which he finds the model within himself. The self-centered man <note>original: "l'homme personnel." This refers to someone focused entirely on their own ego and immediate physical existence rather than spiritual or collective goals.</note> who limits his own duration—bringing everything back to such a tiny point and such a short span of time—makes the part into the whole and the whole into a mere part; he stifles the principle of genius within himself and ends by becoming its enemy, just as he is already the enemy of society.

One may judge, then, by this, how much moral opinions influence the public good <note>original: "la chose publique." A term referring to the common wealth or the shared interests of a community.</note>, and what is owed to those writers whose every phrase cuts a knot of the social bond, and whose works, similar to the siphons used in physics <note>In the 18th century, the siphon was a common scientific demonstration of how pressure could draw liquid out of a vessel; here, it serves as a metaphor for materialistic philosophy draining the human spirit.</note>, tend to pump out and dry up every principle of greatness within us.

->THE MANUAL<-

...I said to myself: Since there is a particular regime <note>original: "régime." In this context, it refers to a "way of life," a set of guiding principles, or a spiritual diet intended to maintain the health of the soul.</note> for every species, man must only find well-being by conforming to the regime of his own; and the regime that provides constant well-being to a single man is the only one that can provide it to all men, since they are all of the same species.

But to whom shall I turn to teach me this beneficial regime? When I ask one man what he believes can make his happiness, he speaks to me of an object which, though possessed by a hundred others, does not make them any happier for it; and my own experience having already taught me that the torment of this kind of desire increases as one satisfies them, I conclude that it would be wise for me to content myself with what I could least afford to lose.

I therefore set myself to study moderation <note>A central virtue in Enlightenment philosophy, often contrasted with the "passions."</note> in the books of the philosophers, in the opulence of the rich, and in the successes and setbacks of the ambitious; and I soon came to believe myself moderate and just, yet I was still seeking happiness. Then, whenever I made a false step, I grew vexed with wisdom <note>original: "la sagesse." The narrator personifies Wisdom as a guide that he intermittently follows or blames.</note>, as if it were she who had made it, or as if, by being unfaithful to her, the fault could be...

<summary>The narrator reflects on the necessity of a universal "regime" or way of life suited to human nature, eventually seeking happiness through a study of moderation across different social strata.</summary>

> "You have everything

> you deserve their crown through the learned dissertations

But let us not confuse the wise man through ignorance with the wise man through experience: the first has not yet given his consent to his laws; not being disillusioned regarding false goods, he can be corrupted, and even corrupts himself when frequenting the world. I go further, and I add that it is possible for one who, disgusted with the world and a momentarily malevolent <note>original: "mal-faiſante."</note> society, has circumscribed the universe within the boundaries of his own self; unhappy in his feeling and the irresolution of his spirit, and appearing, even to his own eyes, worse than he ever was, to find himself, without his knowledge, so advanced in his modification <note>As established on the previous page, "modification" refers to the spiritual process of being shaped and refined by life’s trials.</note>, that a single look toward eternity, bringing his law of the infinite back to its true orient <note>The "true orient" refers to the spiritual East, the source of divine light and the correct alignment of the soul.</note>, might place him on the path of happiness, and lead him to his salvation.

THE MANUAL

## XX.

It is only from ignorance of the nature of their being that philosophers derive their objections against providence, as well as everything they say to prove that man is not free.

Man is free, but it is the feeling of his laws, it is experience, and it is reminiscences <note>reminiscences: Likely referring to the Platonic concept of *anamnesis*, where the soul recovers innate knowledge of divine truths.</note> that must teach him to choose the best; and as he is always rewarded when he chooses well, and always punished when he chooses poorly, he will rise, or fall, or drift, according to what he has deserved and what he has freely chosen. One might try in vain to contest his freedom, but this game of the mind <note>original: "jeu d'eſprit"</note> will never persuade anyone that the person who robs him or strikes him does not do so freely; he will cry thief, or murderer; and if justice is not rendered to him, he will not question whether the thief and the judge violated, freely or by force, the laws of harmony and the laws of convention <term>laws of convention: Man-made legal and social rules, as opposed to the natural or divine "laws of harmony."</term>. Man is therefore a slave only to the necessity of seeking happiness; and everything that happens to him in this search, when he chooses poorly, is only so that he may learn the true

<vocab>providence, freedom, laws of harmony, laws of convention, happiness</vocab>

<summary>The author argues that human freedom is a self-evident reality confirmed by experience and moral accountability, dismissing philosophical denials of free will as mere intellectual games. He asserts that suffering resulting from poor choices serves as a corrective lesson in the pursuit of true happiness.</summary>
<keywords>free will, providence, moral law, harmony, social convention, accountability, pursuit of happiness</keywords>

# OF XÉFOLIUS:

use and the good use he must and can make of his freedom. It is for lack of knowledge concerning the nature and destiny of his being that philosophers have presented him to us, some as perfectly free, others as perfectly enslaved; whereas he is a creature in the crucible of experience and reflection, where he must enlighten himself on the feeling of his duties—which he cannot stifle in his heart, no matter what he does.

### XXI.

Observers, having noticed that the men of their century and their country had lost the frankness, the cordiality, the warmth, and the energy of their ancestors, and that a cold personality <note>original: "froide perſonnalité"; in this context, it implies a detached individualism or self-interest rather than a lack of emotion.</note> was their distinguishing characteristic, sought its cause. They asked if this revolution <note>The author uses "revolution" here in the sense of a complete turning or fundamental change in social character.</note> should be attributed to the progress made by the arts and sciences.

It is true that the question was not proposed in these exact terms; but it is the observation I report that gave rise to it: thus its origin is already a kind of solution.

The Feeling of Harmony <term>Feeling of Harmony: Likely a personification of the intuitive moral sense or a specific philosophical school of thought the author favors.</term> therefore proposed that the arts and sciences had done much—

# LE MANUEL

reflections, one also sees that it is by means of these arts and sciences that they have shaken off the yoke of theological tyrants <note>The author refers here to oppressive religious authorities who restricted intellectual freedom during earlier centuries.</note>, and that they have enlightened themselves regarding the great errors that cause great crimes to be committed and lead to great social discord.

### X X I I.

We all set out from the same point to reach the same circumference through different radii, and it is from the diversity of the types <note>The author likely refers to "types" as spiritual molds or previous states of existence.</note> we have used that the diversity of men's inclinations toward their first prototype arises. ...weak through ignorance, and the good man through principle as much as by feeling? Both often possess an uncertainty in their bearing, and a timidity and embarrassment in their expression. The first commits lowly acts; knowing he does so, he allows himself to be led into blameworthy actions, and is always punished by shame and remorse: the second is easy-going, accommodating, and selfless; but he more willingly makes sacrifices for the lowly than for the great, and never acts against his conscience. This, then, is the touchstone that gives them their true title.

There is no event, encounter, reading, or even a remarkable dream from which we cannot draw <term>modifying reflections</term> <note>original: "réflexions modificatrices." ### XXIII.

The majority of men, emerging from benign species—those who have already utilized several <term>prototypes</term> <note>In this context, "prototypes" likely refers to the various spiritual forms or archetypal stages the soul inhabits during its long journey of refinement.</note>—combine a great gentleness and a profound sensitivity with a great love for harmony. But since they can nonetheless still wander and go astray, it would be well for each person to ask themselves: "Am I happy?" From "why" to "why," from memory to memory, there are many who would find a time, a disposition of the soul, a <term>dawn</term> <note>original: "un orient." While it literally means "the East," in mystical writing it refers to a state of origin, spiritual light, or the "rising" of one's true purpose.</note> where they were less oppressed and less unhappy than they are currently, even though they are now more elevated and wealthier than they were then.

But where do the laws of your being stand? Do your desires harmonize with the interest of the greatest number? Whom do you love? The wicked man fills you with indignation; but what becomes of your indignation when he distinguishes you with favor, or when he is favorable to your views? With what eye do you look back upon the friends you have surpassed, and what is the character of the people who have replaced those friends? How do you speak of your relatives who make no mark in the world? How do you welcome them? For whom do you give yourself the trouble of <term>putting on a mask</term> <note>original: "grimacer." Literally "to grimace," but here referring to the social performance or "faces" one makes to please the powerful or maintain a persona.</note>

<vocab>prototypes, harmony, sensitivity, oppressed, laws of being</vocab>

<summary>This section encourages readers to look past their current wealth and status to see if they have lost the spiritual peace and "harmony" they once possessed in a humbler state. It poses a series of pointed questions about social hypocrisy and how we treat those less successful than ourselves.</summary>

You believe that you were happy because you were young; you are mistaken: without a sense of **harmony**, there is no happiness. The young man who arrives in a state of discord is not happy; driven to mistrust by the awareness of his own vices, and made timid by the fear of being exposed, he does not dare to surrender to trust. He can only numb himself with purely physical pleasures or the thoughts of a pride that is frequently humiliated. 

But your own timidity came from your goodness; you were happy because you were trusting, simple, benevolent, and compassionate. However, one very remarkable thing is that the more the system of discordant selfishness gains credit, the more people desert these circles—in which they no longer believe in harmonious virtues—to form associations where the heart finds room to expand. But, alas! simplicity, gentleness, benevolence, amiable and tender virtues; the leaven and cement of friendship and harmony, you are always strangled...

If, in this journal, there were a column labeled *Pure Pleasures*, they would laugh at it as an absurdity; but *pleasures mixed with anxieties, dull pleasures, tormenting desires, hopes and terrors, deceived hopes, stinging remorse, overwhelming fears, boredom, impatience, jealousies, anger, rages, envy, vengeances, despair, insomnia, fainting spells:* all of this would seem like common sense to them.

To ape the virtues that one has no intention of practicing, to offer what one does not want to give and what ought not be accepted, to mask the selfishness of a cruel soul with the appearance of delicacy and gallantry, to never fail in customary etiquette; this is what constitutes the social grace <note>original: "savoir vivre." In this period, ambition was often viewed as a dangerous vice of the soul rather than a positive trait.</note>; he is a man of the highest company. But the wretch who steals a crown <note>original: "écu," a historical French coin.</note>, he is called a thief, a monster. The former possesses titles and ribbons <note>These refer to the sashes and medals of chivalric orders worn by the nobility.</note>, and feigns dignity...

Do we not see daily how much homage is received by the man who enjoys a certain power and is capable of abusing his means? And how a sad solitude forms around the man who is merely loved and esteemed?

### XXVI.

A fop <note>original: "fat"; a vain, superficial man preoccupied with his social standing.</note> of ancient Rome, finding himself at a festival, asked his freedman <note>original: "affranchi"; a former slave who has been legally granted freedom but often remained in the service of his former master.</note>: *Am I having a good time?* I presume that he would have answered him: "Your bliss, my lord, excites the envy of the gods themselves." A fop of our own day would not ask this question, because the fashion of having freedmen has passed, and because by asking it of others, he would reveal the secret consolation for his boredom, and would betray his entire happiness, which consists only in the thought that he is an object of envy.

Dear Philotheus <note>The name "Philothée" literally means "lover of God" or "friend of God," a common name in philosophical dialogues to represent a student or seeker of truth.</note>, if you are infatuated with this contagious error, you will learn in this work that nothing is more contrary to what you seek; that tranquility of mind is the first stage of this great good, and that it is impossible to acquire it as long as one attaches any value to the judgments of the misguided, even if that which they have at their disposal were of a nature relevant to the infinite need of an immortal being. Ah! If he to whom fortune has given the necessities of life made, in order to make himself happy, half the effort he makes to appear so, what harmony would reign among men! How the palaces of kings and those of ministers would be deserted, and how their majestic solitude would bear witness to wisdom!

<header>THE MANUAL</header>

The Grand Architect <note>A term for God common in Enlightenment philosophy and Freemasonry, emphasizing a universe designed with rational, mathematical order.</note> has combined all the harmonies in such a way that at the very moment you conceive a project of expansion <note>original: "agrandiſſement"; here meaning the expansion of one's estate, power, or social reach.</note>, agitation takes hold of your soul; and he has further willed that constraints <note>original: "ſujétions"; the obligations, burdens, and loss of liberty that come with managing great affairs.</note> and accidents <note>original: "accidens"; unpredictable events or misfortunes that disrupt human plans.</note> of every kind should be the inseparable companions of your undertakings and your successes.

If there is more evil than good on the earth, it is not so for the person who—both morally and physically—living in accordance with the nature of his being, never strives to plunder or to enslave his fellow men; and as the remedy is man’s friend, should you complain that anxiety, fear, remorse, boredom, pain, and grief precede, accompany, and follow your discordant projects? Or should you complain when, from the direction toward which you run, there come blows that warn you to change your route and to seek your happiness in another way?

Being able to occupy only a single point <note>A philosophical concept suggesting that a human being is physically limited to one place and one moment, regardless of how much land they own.</note>, man can do nothing but make exchanges. Yet what pains does he not take to ensure that pains never fail him! Examining his incoherent pretensions, it is evident that, even if one sacrificed the entire human race to him, one could not make the happiness of a single man; this little vessel <note>original: "contenant"; literally 'that which contains,' referring here to the human body or soul as a limited container.</note> would, step by step, wish to swallow the entire universe; but as none of the things he procures can satisfy the nature of his being, he does nothing but draw up with effort, and spill with regret.

Certainly, if the Grand Seigneur <note>A title used in Europe to refer to the Ottoman Sultan.</note> could increase his faculties so as to be able to enjoy his entire seraglio <note>original: "sérail"; the secluded living quarters for the wives and concubines of the Sultan; a harem.</note> all at once, I would understand why it is so numerous; but two harmonious lovers, who husband their pleasures, taste more bliss in a single day than the most powerful monarch of Asia in an entire year. It is the same with everything that exceeds our faculties <note>In 18th-century philosophy, "faculties" refers to the physical, sensory, or mental powers of an individual.</note>. ### XXVIII.

Though it is a fact that all states perish through luxury, how would those whose only resources lie in their talents and industry survive if there were no luxury anywhere? But a government may try in vain to close its doors to it; when the time has come, it will introduce itself no less, to play its ordinary role. Thus, the effect of everything that has been written about this seductive vice <note>The original French word is truncated at the page break: "def-" likely for "défaut" (vice or defect).</note>—

->XXIX.<-

When you are sufficiently advanced in your modification <note>As noted on page 111, "modification" refers to the specific state or level of a person's spiritual and intellectual development.</note> to be warned by your own feelings that the object so much pursued is empty of substance, why yield to false shame and enter the fray with those who can still play the full game of discord? Are you unaware that it is from the lack of reaction found by the sensitive man that his timidity and misanthropy arise, and that the more he acquires knowledge of the world, the less he adapts to its tone?
