<meta>This page continues the thought from page 114 regarding the "price" of worldly involvement, specifically warning that those who seek solitude must be prepared for the lingering effects of the society they leave behind.</meta>

<page-num>115</page-num>

<header>CONCERNING XEFOLIUS. 115</header>

that if he leaves solitude to escape a moment of languor <note>The French *langueur* implies a state of listless boredom or a spiritual weariness that one might feel when alone.</note>, he must expect the obligations, the foolishness, the quarrels, and the gossip of society, the long trails of which will still attempt to disturb him long after he has renounced them.

->XXXI.<-

If you do not know how to be poor and devoted to being forgotten <note>original: "dévoué à l'oubli"; a common theme in 18th-century spiritualism, suggesting a deliberate choice to remain obscure and uncelebrated by the world.</note> without weakness and without ostentation, the edifice of your happiness will crumble on one side while you scaffold it on the other, and you will spend your life in labor, anxiety, and pain.

When your valet has forgotten to put oil in your lamp, or to make a fire for you, say to yourself: *But what if I had no valet? what if I had no oil? what if I had no wood?* and so on for everything else; and you will find a treasure of patience and contentment in this precept, because it is of a harmonious nature, and, being more fortunate than those who conduct themselves according to discordant maxims, it will make you avoid anger and its consequences, by which they are tormented. In the moral as in the physical realm, the nature of one thing acts upon the nature of another, according as these

# ABOUT XEFOLIUS

In my former state of confusion <note>original: "égarement"; refers to a state of being lost, distracted, or wandering away from the truth.</note>, I found friction everywhere; today, everything smiles upon me, everything pleases me, and everything welcomes me.

When someone imagines they would be virtuous if only virtue were honored and rewarded, they are as mistaken about the nature of virtue as they are about the nature of the things they desire. Since, in that hypothetical case, they would be virtuous only for the same reasons they are currently not <note>The author argues that practicing "virtue" solely for the sake of reward is simply another form of the self-interest that prevents one from being truly virtuous in the first place.</note>, their happiness would gain very little from it.

->XXXII<-

Should a simple man imagine that it matters to his salvation to sprinkle a few drops of water on his forehead every morning, he would do well not to miss it, because the remorse from this negligence could—by making him reason correctly about an innocent ritual <note>original: "formule"; here referring to a prescribed religious act or set of words.</note>—lead him to confuse it with the core essence <note>original: "le fond"; the fundamental substance or inner truth of a religion, as opposed to its outward "form."</note>; and it is by this core, necessary to the nature of his being, that he is happy when he holds to it, and unhappy when he detaches from it.

Undoubtedly, man also creates for himself reasons for remorse; but in this case, remorse only comes to him from the thought of having done what he believes to be an evil; and since that is what he must never do, the feeling punishes him for having acted against his conscience. All our movements <note>In the philosophical language of this period, "movements" refers to the internal impulses, desires, or stirrings of the soul.</note> stem from necessary principles, and only yield discordant consequences when we apply them wrongly. ->XXXIII.<-

THE SOLDIER employs his mind and his feeling to study the tactics and the metaphysics <note>original: "métaphysique"; here meaning the underlying theory or abstract principles of warfare.</note> of his art; the historian to discover and bring to light the facts of which he must speak; the poet to paint the passions and their effects through the most enchanting figures and expressions; each, finally, cultivates that from which he hopes for happiness, and all can combine a broad mind with exquisite judgment regarding that which occupies them. ->XXXV.<-

**THE** people of the court, whom moralists <term>moralists: 18th-century writers and thinkers who focused on the study of human character and social behavior.</term> commonly regard as the most corrupt of men, differ from others only in their haughty manners and the tone required by their existence in that place of impostures. This one did not die; he lived, but only to poison the life and cause the death of this father, who had been led astray by his fortune and the love of his own name. <note>The author uses Philémon’s obsession with his "name" (legacy) to illustrate how ego can transform natural fatherly love into a source of suffering.</note>

Just as political systems that encroach upon nature necessarily contain scouring <term>scouring: The original French "détersifs" refers to a medicinal cleansing that can be harsh or corrosive; here it suggests that unnatural systems inevitably purge themselves through painful internal decay.</term> evils; likewise **Philémon**, encroaching upon society and persuaded that his plan and projects were full of wisdom, brought upon himself the private sorrows from which he died. Just as the ambition of a private individual, while leading him to fortune, leads him to evils he would never have experienced had he been more moderate; likewise the ambition of a government, while making it dominant, brings upon it plagues that will cause its ruin. What have we gained from the conquest of America? <note>This likely refers to the ongoing 18th-century debates regarding the "Mirage of the West"—the idea that American colonies brought more war and inflation to Europe than actual wealth.</note> Since then, what has not been written against

->XXXIX.<-

THE wicked man is only distinctly affected by what threatens his physical **sensitivity** <note>In the 18th-century context, "sensibilité" refers to the capacity for physical sensation and emotional response.</note> and his pride. His remorses seem to be reduced to the fear of humiliation, pain, and death. His ordinary state is one of restlessness and unease; he is always running and hoping for happiness. Even desire, which is a kind of enjoyment for those who are less strayed, is a torment for him, because in waiting, he sees only the delay of the remedy for his ailment and the prolongation of his moral suffering. His extraordinary state is a state of fear, agitation, and fury; he would perish in it if it could last, and that is why, when this moment of crisis has passed, he finds himself happy in his habitual position. However, every time events brought about by the interplay of <term>universal laws: The rational, inescapable rules governing the moral and physical world, often synonymous with divine justice in Enlightenment thought.</term> recall his infractions to the memory of society—as this never fails to happen from period to period—he cannot help but still feel the effects of that which had placed him in this violent state.

<sig>I</sig>

But marvel here at the effect of individual **harmony** bound to all other harmonies; for if this man, currently so discordant, were to become harmonious, he would find himself sheltered from the consequences of his infractions, seeing as the modifying influences only act upon the inhabitants of the various regions of discordance.

Universal **religion**, **philosophy**, or what is the same thing—the **truth** that comes to enlighten the guilty—fills him with **remorse** only to heal him; these are caustics <note>A "caustic" is a substance that burns or corrodes organic tissue; the author uses it here as a medical metaphor for a painful but necessary treatment used to clear away "dead" or numb moral tissue.</note> applied to parts whose numbness hides a mortal principle.

However, until he is completely harmonious, how many **revolutions** <note>In this 18th-century philosophical context, "revolutions" refers to cycles of existence, stages of spiritual progress, or the turning of fate's wheel.</note> will he not have to traverse again! How many sorrows, how many sufferings will not already come to weaken him in the one he is traversing now, and whose cause it would be quite difficult for him to recognize in those very infractions that promised him so much **happiness**!

->XL.<- <note>Roman numeral 40, marking a new section.</note>

If the **universe** had not been confined to our tiny globe, to an Elysium, or to a Tartarus <note>The paradisiacal and infernal realms of Greek mythology, used here to represent limited religious views of the afterlife.</note>, the whole thing surrounded by candles <note>A mocking reference to a small-minded view of the cosmos, perhaps comparing the stars to altar candles or stage lights.</note>, we would have been more just toward **God** and men.

Man seeks revenge either because he believes he has been wronged, or to prove that he is not to be trifled with; that is to say, he seeks revenge only out of greed and fear, while believing he acts out of a sense of justice.

Now, everyone knows to what excesses our discordant passions can drive us. But the **Eternal**, who is beyond the reach of our attacks—the Eternal, who is as good as He is just—exercises His justice only in equal measure with His goodness. Since His goodness created us for a happy end, He has justly ordered the nature of things in such a way that:

1. No crime can remain unpunished;
2. Punishment becomes, sooner or later, a source of enlightenment for the offender and for many others;
3. We cannot displace or infringe upon our laws without falling into a **state of ill-being** proportioned to our infraction and to the <term>moral dislocation</term> <note>original: "luxation morale." In Sparta, they used to make the Helots <note>original: "Ilotes." In ancient Sparta, the Helots were a subjugated class of people; citizens would famously force them to drink to excess so that Spartan youths could witness the degrading effects of drunkenness and learn to avoid it.</note> drunk to disgust the youth with such a dangerous drink. Do not say: "I will permit myself this anti-harmonious pleasure, and I accept the penalty that must follow"; for as evil, like good, is linked by a chain of events impossible to foresee, you will not get off with only the foreseen penalty, and you will repent for the bad bargain you have made.

We cannot deviate from the path of harmony without falling into the shadowy space of uncertainties, from which arise only sighs, vain regrets, and deceptive hopes, and where people clash and tear one another apart.

### XLV.

EVERYTHING the Great Architect <note>Great Architect: A common Enlightenment-era term for God or a Divine Creator, often used in Deist philosophy to describe a rational designer of the universe.</note> has made is necessary just as it is; everything is linked, everything is held together, everything tends equally toward the propagation, the preservation, and the happy end of beings. To one pain, one can add a second, then a third; a man can ultimately be made to die in this progression. But it is not the same for pleasure; there is nothing to be added to the greatest of all those we know, and it cannot be repeated at will, for the reason that, if man—who is endowed with a need for infinite happiness—could dispose of pleasure as he disposes of pain, and extend it likewise in the heat of his passions, he would dissolve at his first enjoyment, and with him all his posterity would vanish. What would become of him if the fear of pain and death were not charged with watching over his preservation? What would become of society if pain had only the same limits as pleasure? What would the wicked man not dare then? But fear is to nature

The law of the infinite, by giving us the sense of our immortality, causes the misguided man <note>original: "le dévoyé," referring to someone who has wandered from the path of reason or virtue</note>, even at the very edge of his grave, to still feast on chimeras <note>chimeras: Illusions or impossible dreams</note>, and to form projects whose execution would require centuries of life and a series of successes no less impossible.

->XLVII.<-

**WE** command ourselves to show a feigned warmth to praise the ostentatious man, but we truly love the benevolent man. We seek to scrutinize the motives that drove the former to act, and we feel that to do good, the latter required nothing more than the opportunity. We wish to be settled <note>original: "être quittes," meaning to have repaid a debt of gratitude so as to no longer feel beholden to someone</note> with the first, but gratitude toward the latter will weigh on us all the less because he is more harmonious; and since it is a fact that, all things being nearly equal, our laws and our recollections lead us to help by preference the unfortunate person most related to us (because we anticipate the pleasure of the reaction <note>reaction: In this context, the author refers to the emotional feedback or reciprocal sentiment that follows an act of kindness</note> with which he will repay our kindness), our gratitude becomes a source of reactive pleasures for ourselves.

### ABOUT XEFOLIUS

and for the benefactor worthy of love and esteem, as long as pride or a false opinion on the nature of things does not come to dry up this source and lead either of us astray into the deserts of arid selfishness. If the great <note>In the 18th-century context, "the great" refers to the aristocracy or those with high social and political status.</note> were less imperious and less self-centered, the small—being then able to love them—would show less ingratitude; but when they debase those they have helped, and demand from them boundless patience and devotion, are their benefits not like snares <note>original: "rets," meaning nets or traps used for hunting or fishing</note> that pride has cast toward misfortune? What sense of the beautiful must one possess not to be more indignant than grateful in such a case?

But since you should no more fear suspicion and criticism than you should desire praise, and since there is just as much vanity in hiding oneself as in showing oneself, do good wherever you find the opportunity; and if malice attributes corrupt motives to you, respond only by continuing to do good.

Listen no longer to the reproaches leveled against the poor by those people who, in order to excuse themselves from helping, would sell their country and everything they claim to respect, if they could do so without risk. While waiting for the government...

### XLVIII.

PEOPLE greedy for consideration <note>considération: Social standing or the esteem of others.</note>, without knowing what it is, balanced upon their own consistency <note>meaning their social standing or importance</note>, like an acrobat <note>original: "baladin," a street performer or buffoon</note> on a rope—do they see an end to this violent and perilous state, or do they flatter themselves that happiness will come to nest beside them? ### XLIX.

**LOVE** is the strongest of the passions; because it promises happiness to our two natures, and because it takes hold of our entire being at once. Whatever the misguided may think, its principal charm, its sweetest charm, comes from the nature of destiny; for it will not be for the most beautiful woman that a well-born <note>original: "heureusement né," implying someone born with a good disposition or noble character.</note> young man will become passionate, but rather for her whose countenance best expresses the feelings of the laws of this nature. This charm is so penetrating, so celestial, that, whatever the ardor of one's temperament, it can even overrule <note>original: "surprendre," here meaning to overtake or catch off guard.</note> the desires of the transient nature; and if the latter finally enjoys its rights, it is only ever as a subordinate to the power of the former. <note>The author distinguishes between "transient nature" (physical or fleeting desire) and the "nature of destiny" (spiritual or essential connection).</note>

He who has loved well only once in his life has gone astray early on, if he has chosen for himself a chimera <note>A fancy or an illusion.</note> less related to the nature of our being. One is inconstant in love only because one has not found in the object—

->L.<-

Since we are compelled to seek happiness, and forced to obey the object from which we hope to obtain it; he whose law of the infinite is attached to the earth can only be a conditional friend. <note>The author argues that if a person's values are purely material or "earthly," their friendship will always be dependent on what they can gain from it.</note>

### THE MANUAL

...reason; for, to be capable of friendship, the laws of harmony must speak so loudly that one already knows, at the very least, that happiness recedes and diminishes in proportion to how much one offends them. <note>The author posits that friendship is not merely a social bond but a spiritual one governed by "laws of harmony." To be a true friend, one must understand that immoral actions disrupt this harmony and decrease one's own happiness.</note>

There are those who feel these laws intensely, and owe the greatest part of their enjoyment to this feeling; but led astray by the mind <note>original: "l’esprit"; here referring to the intellectual or rationalizing faculty which can sometimes override natural moral intuition.</note>, and neither reflecting nor studying this feeling enough, they hope for and expect their happiness from many objects which they will not sacrifice to their friendship for you—even though this friendship will often be sincere, disinterested, and have no other cause than the pleasure resulting from the interplay of corresponding laws. It is therefore only upon the knowledge you have of your friend’s harmony that you can calculate the degree of reliance <note>original: "le fond"; refers to the depth of character or the "foundation" of trust one can place in someone.</note> you can place in him. If a part of his mind is divided from his feeling, and if that feeling is not of extraordinary warmth and vigor, it will not take a major interest for him to falter, to abandon you, or to betray you <note>original: "perfider"; an archaic usage meaning to act with perfidy or break one's word.</note>; the fear of losing, or the hope of gaining, a tiny fragment of happiness, will be enough for that.

### L I I.

BY confounding the accessories with the principal, men imagine they can make themselves happy in their own way, as if they did not live under the dominion of the invariable laws of nature, the effects of which they can no more oppose than they can the circulation of the blood. When they offend the laws of their transient nature <note>original: "nature passagère"; likely referring to the physical body and its biological needs.</note>, they are not surprised by the illnesses that follow, and have recourse to remedies; but when they sin against the laws of their destined nature <note>original: "nature destinée"; referring to the moral or spiritual purpose of a human being, or the soul's intended path.</note>, they are quite surprised to find themselves unhappy, and prefer not to believe in this nature, rather than submit to the regimen indicated by the harmony of reason and sentiment, and which they

To know our duties is science; to fulfill them without any other motive than the interest of feeling is strength; to always consult the harmonious point before acting is wisdom; to live and die in accordance with our laws—that is virtue, the secret of happiness and salvation.

->LV.<-

**FORTUNE** usually does so much harm to those she favors that there are very few who retain enough of those virtues—beneficent to oneself and others—to be forgiven for their prosperity. ### L V I.

THE good you would do for your fellow beings, if you had the means, will be rewarded as if you had actually done it. The rich man has no advantage over you; because we possess nothing, we lack nothing; nothing happens to us that does not tend toward our **modification** <term>modification: in this philosophical tradition, the term refers to the internal transformation or spiritual refinement of the soul's nature through life's trials and experiences</term>: our pains and even our pleasures are so many lessons intended to change us.

### L V I I.

**IGNORANCE** asks: Why has **Fortune** come to seek out this wicked man? But, **Wisdom** answers, do you know what experience he is lacking? Wait for the use he will make of his new riches. He who dazzles us with his pomp, and who was the object of our envy, has become the object of our pity before the sun has finished its circuit. Do not say, therefore, that such an event is happy, or that another is unhappy, because you do not know the consequences they [will bring].

### LIX.

"I made him return to himself" <note>original: "rentrer en lui-même"; an idiom for self-reflection, introspection, or coming to one's senses.</note> is an expression as old as time, and one whose meaning has great depth. Do they wish to prove their innocence to a public that suspects them? They will say: *I am at peace, my conscience reproaches me for nothing.* They also sometimes speak of the death of the righteous; but they stop there, only to occupy themselves with things that will not procure for them that happy death.

### LX.

**When** you have stumbled <note>original: "bronché"; literally to trip or lose one's footing, used here as a metaphor for committing a moral error or mistake.</note>, do not become angry with yourself, or with the person who provided the occasion for it, for this anger comes only from a spirit of pride; but say to yourself: I stumbled for such and such a reason; I will be careful another time; I am going to repair and redeem my fault as best as I can. If it is a misfortune to fall, it is a crime not to pick oneself up properly.

### LXI.

**As** our laws have action and reaction, and since shared pleasure increases while pain diminishes—both being communicated by the virtue of our essence <term>essence: in this philosophical context, it refers to the fundamental spiritual nature of a human being.</term> and the interplay of our laws—be less presumptuous, and you will be more indulgent toward yourself and others. Pride is severe and repellent; love sympathizes and attracts; and since the law of love is in the heart of all men, make your own vibrate, and theirs will quiver.

<vocab>laws, the Eternal, pride, law of love, essence, indulgent</vocab>

### LXII.

**The** sufferings that we have not brought upon ourselves through infractions advance the work of man; those we have deserved prepare new ones for us, if we do not learn from them <note>original: "si nous n'en profitons pas"; literally "if we do not profit from them," suggesting that even deserved suffering serves a purpose if used for self-improvement.</note>.

But the disciple of harmony must not resemble the fierce devotee, who would do evil if evil were not punished, and who gnaws at the irons by which fear holds him enchained. This man may well not sin in deed, yet the dispositions in which he lives will nonetheless lead him back to the crucible <note>original: "creuset"; a vessel used for melting metals at high temperatures to remove impurities, here symbolizing a process of painful spiritual purification.</note>, because no one enters, nor can enter, the realms of harmony until he is so harmonious with love that he no longer owes his purity and his harmonious conduct to fear or to hope, but to his feeling modified <term>modification: in this philosophical school, this refers to the internal transformation or "tuning" of the soul's character to reach a higher state of being.</term> to the ultimate degree, according to the present faculties of our being. This does not exclude desire, fear, and hope, which man cannot and must not be able to cast off; the difference consists in the fact that the devotee is restrained and guided only by these three motives, whereas they are merely reinforcements <note>original: "reconfortans"; things that strengthen or provide support.</note> for the disciple of harmony who is advanced in his modification.

<summary>This section distinguishes between those who avoid sin out of fear of punishment and the "disciple of harmony" who acts out of a transformed, loving nature. It explains that true spiritual progress requires moving beyond the external motivations of fear and hope to an internal state of being.</summary>

### LXIII.

**INDIGNATION** is the anger of the righteous. Only the friend of Domitian <note>Titus Flavius Domitianus (51–96 AD), a Roman Emperor frequently characterized by historians as a cruel and paranoid tyrant.</note> and the enemy of Titus <note>Titus Flavius Vespasianus (39–81 AD), remembered as a benevolent and virtuous Emperor, often called the "Delight of Mankind."</note> could ignore the power of virtue, or contest everything that the harmonious man is capable of sacrificing for the social order.

Where is the ambitious man who, when his slightest private interest demands it, does not cause great damage to the general interest? Before trying to persuade me that you are a zealous servant of society, prove to me that you are not its treacherous enemy; before claiming to be useful to it, start by no longer harming it. Are there any who would subscribe to this condition? No, there are none. Those who are led astray by principle, acting only out of corrupt interests, are convinced that no man can act according to the sense of the laws of our being. They have entirely displaced these laws when, in their ignorance of human nature, they derived monstrous systems of discordant egoism from the natural love of self <note>The author distinguishes between "amour de soi" (a natural, healthy instinct for self-preservation) and "égoïsme" (selfishness that creates social disharmony).</note>.

### LXIV.

**NO MATTER** how wicked a man may be, he only sins through the spirit <note>original: "l'esprit"; here referring to the intellect, reason, or the "mind" as the source of error, rather than the "heart" or the core of one's being.</note>, and not through the heart.

How many people do we see in the world making themselves look ridiculous through a way of being they believe to be the best, and from which they hope for esteem and admiration—that is to say, their ideal happiness? Without doubt, he who dies with many reminiscences <term>reminiscences; a concept likely drawn from Platonic "anamnesis," suggesting that the soul carries fragments of memory or "light" from previous states of existence or higher realms</term> of sentiment is reborn more sensitive and more loving than he who has fewer; but these reminiscences are lights acquired within the types and prototypes <note>The author uses "types and prototypes" to refer to archetypal forms or the foundational structures of reality that the soul interacts with across its existence.</note> that he has already made use of. L 4

Modern philosophers, after having observed the disproportions or inconsistencies between the character and the spirit of man, but not knowing the nature of his being, have made vague distinctions, such as not having the spirit of one’s character, or having too much character for one’s spirit, or too much spirit for one’s character, etc. etc. This, then, is what they have caught a glimpse of: Men, at their first prototype <note>In this context, the "prototype" refers to the original, archetypal state of a human being before they are shaped by worldly experience.</note>—and whose temporary destiny is to be modifiers <note>The author views humans as "modifiers" (modificateurs), beings whose role is to shape or alter their own nature and the world around them during their earthly life.</note>—usually have the character of their spirit; that is to say, their sentiment being little modified, and having only their own laws to violate, they can, without making great efforts, employ all the means proper to procure for themselves the existence where they hope to find the happiness they are compelled to seek.

In all other men, there occur struggles, more or less harsh, between the spirit and sentiment, according to the degree of modification they have reached, compared to the opinions of their spirit on the nature of our being and on the nature of things, as well as the principles according to which they have resolved to conduct themselves. ### LXV.

How are you faring, *Damon*, with the decoration <note>A medal or insignia of honor awarded by a state or monarch.</note> you obtained a year ago? How you desired it! What furies, what intrigues, what plans for vengeance against those you suspected of causing you to miss it the previous year! Yet, of what has it cured you? Are your days more serene for it? Is your sleep calmer and deeper? Do you have a better appetite? Do you digest better? Are you no longer prone to outbursts? Do you no longer desire anything with that same fury? What are your emotions now? Reflect on it, and you will know what you owe to your ribbon <note>original: *cordon* — This refers to the silk ribbon or sash associated with an order of knighthood or a high civil honor, such as the *Cordon Bleu*.</note>, because it is the emotions that constitute the feeling of both happiness and misery; for the sickly, discordant being usually has only painful ones, and cannot experience a single one without it being troubled and weakened by his habitual ones, and by the vapors that exhale from the depths that nourish them.

<vocab>Damon, decoration, ribbon, ambition, emotions, happiness, misery</vocab>

### LXVI.

ONE is mistaken in attributing our waywardness <note>original: *égaremens* — referring to moral or intellectual deviations or errors in judgment.</note> to our fibers, to our blood, to our bile, or finally to some nuance in the prototype <note>In this context, the "prototype" refers to the original biological constitution or "blueprint" of an individual.</note>; these belong only to the errors of the mind. One does not get angry over indifferent subjects, and one always has excellent reasons to offer those people who fly into a passion for things that are not part of our own collection <note>The author uses "collection" here to mean the set of things, values, or interests that an individual personally treasures or deems important.</note>.

Not long ago, you would have sacrificed everything for the very object you disdain today; where does this change come from? It comes from the fact that after having experienced this object, you recognized its powerlessness to give you what you had hoped for. Nevertheless, how many cruel hours it forced you to endure! How many pleasures did you sacrifice to it! How many enemies did it make you! And what influence does this now-destroyed error still exert over your present position and the current state of your soul! Now, the same holds true for whatever is leading you astray at this moment; for <term>self-love</term> <note>original: *amour propre* — in 18th-century philosophy, this term specifically refers to a vanity-driven, comparative self-interest, as opposed to *amour de soi*, which is a natural instinct for self-preservation.</note>, as fertile in unfortunate results as it is in illusions—the surface of which <term>M. de la Rochefoucault</term> <note>François de La Rochefoucauld (1613–1680), a French author famous for his "Maximes," which explored how hidden vanity and ego (*amour-propre*) motivate human actions.</note> has admirably depicted—is at its core nothing but the love of ourselves, which can never abandon us, which takes on different forms, and produces...

At the <term>theater</term> <note>original: *spectacle* — in 18th-century philosophy, the theater was often used as a laboratory for observing human nature and communal emotion.</note>, where there is something for everyone, where the interest of one does not oppose that of another—where, when the author and the actor remain within the truth, the action and reaction of the laws of our being seem to double the sentiment of each individual and blend the souls of the spectators—in this delicious union, we are all in agreement on the force that deserves praise or blame, admiration or contempt, esteem or hatred.

### LXXI.

HOW many truths the man of the whirlwind <note>original: *l’homme du tourbillon* — A common 18th-century metaphor for a person caught up in the chaotic, fast-paced life of high society or public affairs.</note> recognizes by his fireside—truths which, once returned to society, he abjures by his conduct and his speech, and which he would not even dare to confess! Left to himself, his laws make themselves heard, they call him back to order; his good spirit <note>original: *le bon génie* — Here referring to one's conscience or inner moral guide.</note> inspires salutary thoughts; the play of universal laws has brought about events worthy of reflection, and the sweetness of harmony makes itself felt.

### LXXII.

**VICES** are easily communicated: 1st, because they are tied to fashionable opinions <note>original: *opinions de bon ton* — the prevailing trends and social standards of high society.</note>; 2nd, because they promise us both fortune and pleasures; 3rd, because, to adopt the virtues, or not to be unfaithful to them, the intellect must be as convinced as the heart <note>original: *le sentiment* — here referring to inner moral intuition or feeling as opposed to cold logic.</note> is often persuaded that virtue alone can ensure our happiness—and that is not what the influential crowd thinks. However, by meditating on one’s past life and present sorrows, everyone would find that virtue deliciously seasons the pleasures it allows us to taste; and that, on the contrary, it poisons (either in the moment or in their consequences) those that eternal justice forbids us, even the most innocent, when, in order to procure them, we offend the harmony.

### LXXIII.

Men pass like shadows. Tomorrow it will be forgotten that you ever lived, or whatever is said of you will no longer affect you. In a state of greater development <note>original: *développement* — likely referring to the soul's progression after death or a higher state of spiritual evolution.</note>, you will be more enlightened and happier than you are now; then, you will compare the great interests of haughty men to the rattles <note>original: *hochets* — literally toys or teething rings; used here to describe the trifles that adults take seriously.</note> of your childhood.

If you are still too weak to trample underfoot some trifle that fortune had given you and that harmony <term>harmony: the cosmic or moral order that governs the universe in the author's philosophy, representing a state of alignment with divine or natural law</term> commands you to sacrifice, turn your mind away from it and think of the life to come.

Allow yourself to reach a state of indifference <note>original: *indolence* — here used in the classical sense of "freedom from pain or passion" (apatheia), rather than the modern sense of laziness.</note> regarding that which only touches you in order to torment you: recall to your memory the number of ambitious men whom death harvests in the middle of their race, and in the bitterness of their lives. The calm that these reflections will bring to your heart will prove to you that you are resting upon the bed of the nature of your being <note>A poetic way of saying that inner peace is the natural and rightful resting place for the human soul.</note>.

But do not mistake for the nonchalance of the wise man that preference which some give to their own laziness and tastes over the duties of their station <note>original: *devoirs de son état* — the specific moral and social obligations tied to one's rank, profession, or position in society.</note>; you must fulfill your own with the most scrupulous exactitude; and when authority opposes the—

Whatever others may then try to blame you for will no longer be able to wound you, for you will be entirely innocent, if, withdrawn into a state of indifference <note>original: *nonchalance* — here meaning a stoic detachment or lack of anxiety regarding external outcomes, rather than laziness.</note>, you have not committed the errors allowed only by vanity or the interests of discord.

Thus, before surrendering yourself to pleasure or any occupation of your own choosing, see if you have any duties left to fulfill, and never put off until another day what you must do right now. Having fulfilled your obligations to society, you will find more enjoyment in your undertakings, and you will succeed in them more effectively.

A different course of conduct drives away calm, and is always punished by events brought about by the operation of universal laws.

Almost all men pitch their tone above their abilities; and although they often have cause to blush for having so poorly lived up to their own display, they do not correct themselves. However, just as one must regulate their expenses below their income to avoid ruin, so too does harmony <term>harmony: a state of inner balance where one's external actions perfectly reflect their internal moral state</term> require that we hold back a portion of our strength wherever we are not forced to exert our full means. To make a parade of one’s power is vanity and injustice; to exercise it over our neighbor is to sin against the precept <term>precept: refers here to the "Golden Rule," a fundamental moral principle found in many traditions</term> which forbids us from doing to others what we would not want done to ourselves. Whatever trouble this treacherous delight <note>original: *perfide délectation* — referring to the fleeting, poisonous pleasure found in exerting power or superiority over others.</note> never fails to bring upon us, sooner or later, is but the just punishment for our transgressions.

### LXXVI.

The man who has been often deceived no longer wishes to rely on anything but his own intellect; but, being at war with the inner assent of truth, he will only become inconsistent and unhappy once he has adopted the principles of selfishness <term>selfishness: translated from *égoïsme*, which in this period referred to a narrow, philosophical focus on the self that neglects social and spiritual duties</term>. Whether he follows his intellect or his heart, he will never be satisfied with himself: either his feelings will sting him with guilt, or his intellect will humble him with doubt. Led astray by the objects of pride and by the maxims of the day,

### LXXVII.

Many naturally follow, especially in their earliest youth, the sentiment of harmony; and these will be true friends until, once engaged in the world, they believe they have acquired enlightenment <term>enlightenment: used here somewhat ironically to refer to the cynical "wisdom" or "social education" of the world rather than spiritual truth</term>, and begin to calculate friendship according to the market rate <note>original: *tarif* — literally a tariff or price list, suggesting that in society, even human connections become transactional.</note> they find established for all things. And what atrocities has the necessity of seeking happiness not caused to be committed throughout all ages! Even the history of religion offers examples that make nature shudder.

# ON XEFOLIUS.

Everyone shrugs their shoulders at what leads another astray, and knows of nothing more important than the object they themselves pursue just as foolishly. This spectacle, where the fool and the man of genius <term>genius: in this context, "genius" (French: *génie*) refers to a person of exceptional natural ability or a high spirit, rather than just modern intellectual brilliance</term> are on the same level, has a singular side when one knows the nature of our being and its progressive modification; because then, not measuring crime and wickedness by the offense done to justice, but by the weakness and wandering of men, one gives oneself over neither to anger nor to vengeance.

And in thinking of all the evils that await the wicked <note>original: *méchant* — this term implies someone who is morally lost or harmful to others</note> to enlighten them, indignation, tempered by benevolent pity, brings us back to the harmonious point; for one only hates and takes revenge because one believes they have been harmed in that from which they hoped for happiness <note>original: *bonheur* — implying a state of total well-being or luck</note>, and because this error makes us suffer. 

From this it follows that objects which appear despicable to us nevertheless have such a great value in the opinion of many others that they daily cause anger, hatred, and vengeance that we cannot conceive; and that we do not lack good reasons to soften and console those who complain of a thing that would perhaps have irritated us even more than them, [had we shared their perspective].

<vocab>genius, justice, indignation, pity, happiness, despicable, vengeance, the wicked</vocab>

# DE XÉFOLI US. <note>This heading appears to be an OCR corruption of a classical name, likely "DE XÉNOPHON."</note>

The favor of the powerful might demand that my friend betray me, or at least that he disown me; he will therefore betray me, he will disown me. <term>Lucullus: a Roman general (c. 118–57 BC) famous for his immense wealth and the lavish, extravagant banquets he hosted</term> might make his table the price for having no connection with me; then my <term>parasite: in this context, a "social parasite"—someone who stays close to the wealthy to enjoy their food and lifestyle</term> friend will distance himself from me, will flee from me. It is not necessary to study a man deeply to know upon what he makes his existence depend: this is always the most prominent feature in men; and if a similarity of tastes is the cause of most connections, it is also the cause of most breakups when it does not have the love of virtue for its foundation. Thus, to dare to decide which of two people bound together loves the least and is the most disposed to break away, one only needs to have studied the needs of one and the other, once one already knows the accidents to which the objects of those needs are exposed, as well as the conduct and the sacrifices required by the acquisition, possession, and preservation of these objects. But after having meditated well on this subject, one will perhaps find that the friendship of people of the world is hardly more solid and more noble than that which reigns between two dogs until a bone is thrown to them.

<summary>The author argues that worldly friendships are inherently unstable because they are based on mutual needs and social gain rather than virtue. He concludes that such loyalties inevitably vanish the moment a person's interests or "needs" are threatened by their associations.</summary>

# THE MANUAL

All these sacrifices to the primary object are made with more or less skill; for however strayed, however depraved one may be, the <term>laws of harmony</term><note>In this context, the "laws of harmony" refers to an innate sense of moral order or conscience that remains even in a corrupt person, prompting them to hide their vices.</note> are never extinguished so deeply within the heart that they no longer make us feel it is prudent to mask from the public eye the motives of criminal and contemptible conduct.

Let us speak a humiliating but great truth: it is that all those who cling primarily to the earth, who seek their happiness in what men control—I do not even exclude their esteem and their praise, which are as unstable as the wind—and who see in these things anything other than more or less indifferent accessories, will be forced to betray friendship whenever their primary object demands it. When one understands this <term>immutable law of necessity</term><note>A philosophical argument that once a person prioritizes worldly gain, their subsequent betrayal of others becomes an inevitable "necessity" of their chosen path.</note>, how can one be surprised by the petty quarrels of the common sort of people of all ranks? These are people who live by chance, who cling to everything without clinging to anything, whose location, circumstances, and surroundings decide their way of being, their mood, and their opinions; having nothing distinct about them except the vagueness of the pretensions of vanity, they simply <term>go where the wind blows</term><note>original: "vau-le-vent," meaning to drift aimlessly or act without a solid moral compass.</note>.

### LXXIX.

THERE are some men who claim to <term>transcharacterize</term><note>French: "transcaractériser." A term likely coined by the author to describe the attempt to fundamentally change one's innate nature or "character" into something entirely different.</note> themselves, while nevertheless intending to remain what they are at heart.

They choose a model for themselves, and believe that by striving to imitate it in certain ways, they will resemble it perfectly; as if the foundation did not correspond to all the parts, and as if what one retains of one’s own character could fail to clash visibly with what one adds from the outside!

We cannot <term>transcharacterize</term> ourselves, and that is fortunate; but we can <term>modify</term><note>The author distinguishes between changing one's essence (impossible) and "modifying" it—a process of refining and aligning one's existing nature with divine laws.</note> ourselves through piety and a great attention to the laws of our being. As soon as man rises through will, feeling, and prayer toward the <term>Great Principle</term><note>French: "le grand Principe." A common Enlightenment-era and mystical term for God or the Divine Source of the universe.</note>, he is particularly protected by it. Therefore, often lift your heart and your thought toward your Father; He will confound the schemes of the wicked who had sworn your ruin, and will make you triumph over the <term>prudence</term><note>French: "prudence." Here used in the sense of worldly cunning or the cold, calculated strategy of enemies.</note> of your enemies.

But let morality speak only through your conduct; communicate your principles only to the fervent friends of truth, whose bearing, tone, and discourse do not cause distress: and do not...

<summary>The author argues that while one's fundamental character cannot be changed, it can be spiritually refined through prayer and adherence to divine laws. He advises living morally through actions rather than words and sharing deep truths only with trusted, kindred spirits.</summary>

### LXXX.

IF I had to speak to the <term>misguided people</term><note>French: "peuple dévoyé." This refers to the masses who have been led astray by worldly distractions and false philosophies.</note>, I would say to them:
> “You have tried the objects of pride long enough to have been enlightened regarding the falsehood and treachery of their promises; try now beneficence and piety, and you will not be slow to feel that these are the nourishment of your nature and your destiny: believe no longer in what men tell you; they do nothing but contradict one another: yield only to the truth that is recognized by the contentment of inner peace, which is the seal that the Eternal has stamped upon it.”

<page-num>199</page-num>
->### CONCERNING XEFOLIUS. <note>Xefolius is likely a character name or a symbolic pseudonym used within this philosophical dialogue to represent a specific moral perspective.</note><-
->N 4<-

...as a cowardice, or to commit an evident crime. Given this, how can one say of such a man that he is weak? Would one say of a man who carries a burden weighing three hundred pounds without difficulty that he lacks strength because he was seen yielding to the impact of a child? No; one would think that he yielded to that child because he believed it was reasonable and just to do so. 

Well then, to lead these supposedly weak individuals to the greatest sacrifices, it is always necessary—and usually sufficient—to succeed in persuading them that the action is praiseworthy; for it is from this class of men that the most ardent and sincere sighs for the <term>beautiful par excellence</term> <note>The "beautiful par excellence" refers to the Platonic ideal of Absolute Beauty, which in this context is synonymous with the highest moral Good or Divine Truth.</note> arise: and if some eventually appear to no longer seek it, it is only because they despair of ever finding it. 

But should they happen to become enlightened and struck by the charms of virtue, they will draw from the very source of their alleged weakness a strength capable of humbling and confounding the most determined of the <term>misguided</term> <note>Original: *dévoyés*. This refers to those who have consciously turned away from the path of truth or morality.</note>. ### LXXXIII.

My physical laws warn me of my physical needs; it is not my mind that awakens the feeling. When an unexpected object threatens my eye, the eyelid does not wait for a <term>syllogism</term> <note>A formal logical argument where a conclusion is reached from two previous premises (e.g., "all men are mortal, I am a man, therefore I am mortal"). Here, the author means the eyelid acts instantly without needing a logical thought process.</note> of the mind to cover it; but the <term>modern Pyrrhonists</term> <note>Followers of Pyrrhonism, a school of extreme skepticism that doubts the possibility of attaining true knowledge. The author uses this to describe people who overthink and doubt natural moral impulses.</note> put all their sagacity into finding a point of discord in the harmonious actions to which we are invited, both by the laws that the <term>Great Architect</term> <note>A common Enlightenment-era and Masonic term for God, depicted as the designer or builder of the universe.</note> has imprinted upon beings destined to live in society, and by the invisible reward he has attached to them.

When the <term>misguided</term> <note>Original: *dévoyés*. Those who have lost their moral or spiritual direction.</note> are invited by these happy laws, it is within their prudence and their strength to reflect on their own self-interest, in order to see if it would not be a weakness to yield to them; then they yield or resist according to whether they perceive a gain or a loss from the discord; but most often the play of universal laws brings about events that make them repent of their false wisdom.

What a gathering of circumstances do they not require to do good! Would they renounce the slightest pleasure to help a stranger of low birth? No, for they would <insert>do</insert> <note>The text ends mid-sentence at the bottom of the page.</note>

Far from the first impulse—which disposes us to help our neighbor, to save him from a displeasure, or to procure for him some pleasure—originating from a feeling of <term>corrupt self-interest</term> <note>The author distinguishes between "natural" interest (doing what is right for the soul) and "corrupt" interest (selfishness or greed).</note>, it is instead inspired in us by the interest of the feeling of harmony. Should a man who has captured his admiration through seemingly generous traits be unmasked, revealing nothing but a hypocritical and greedy soul, he will be painfully affected and revolted by it. This is because he had very clearly felt that man can act through noble and harmonious sentiments; and this pain is not always the humiliation of having been mistaken, as some have claimed...

### LXXXIV.

The wayward man, though backward in his <term>modification</term> <note>In the author's philosophical system, "modification" refers to the progressive refinement of an individual's character and the development of their soul toward a more harmonious state.</note>, can nevertheless have acquired, through the study of the laws of our being, great knowledge of social harmony; and starting from there, he may deliver such beautiful discourses on virtue that even the most clear-sighted person would be fooled, if they were unaware of his conduct and did not observe the spontaneous movements of this man. 

But how is it that one man displays fine principles yet practices bad ones, while another displays bad principles but commonly practices good ones? It is because the first, backward in his modification, admits the truth wherever it is useful to him; and politically, where it is not contrary to his particular interest. But the second, more advanced in his modification, more shaken in his <term>sensibility</term> <note>The capacity to perceive moral truths through feeling rather than just cold logic.</note>, fearing to clash with the opinions of the so-called <term>eagles</term> <note>An ironic reference to self-proclaimed intellectual elites or cynical philosophers who believe they see "further" than the common man.</note>, reasons according to the common errors—

### LXXXV.

LOVE and justice form the foundation of individual harmony and social harmony.

He who trades love and justice for wealth and power, gives away his capacity for enjoyment in exchange for the *means* of enjoyment, and withers away in his excess <note>original: "superflu," referring to material abundance beyond what is necessary for life.</note>.

Since love and justice form the foundation of harmony, true gratitude <note>original: "reconnoissance," an archaic spelling of *reconnaissance*.</note>, as well as true friendship, is subject to their laws; this is why one finds so many ingrates and so few friends. Those whom the world calls *warm friends* <note>original: "amis chauds," referring to those whose friendship is based on passionate impulse or fervor rather than steady moral principle.</note>, no doubt take pleasure in doing

Let us speak plainly: gratitude <note>original: "reconnoissance"; in this context, it implies both the feeling of thankfulness and the acknowledgment of a debt or bond.</note> is but a word imagined by men to express a type of friendship which, like all types of friendship, has infinite nuances. Gratitude derives from friendship, just as vengeance derives from hatred. We see more acts of vengeance than of gratitude only because the former flatter the interests of a spirit of pride, whereas the latter cost it dearly; and man, always driven by his self-interest, is never far from hating the benefactor he cannot love.

However, suppose two thieves rob me; one saves my life when the other wants to take it. Where is the interest of the first? It lies in his law of pity—a law so dear to the wretched and so necessary to the social system <note>original: "systême social"; an Enlightenment concept referring to the natural and moral bonds that hold a society together.</note>. The movement of this law in the thief's heart is felt by all the powers of mine; all my harmonious laws <note>The author uses "harmonious laws" to describe the internal moral and emotional resonances that occur when two people share a moment of genuine humanity.</note>, shuddering at once, react upon his. He is moved to tenderness; at that moment, I love him deeply, our tears mingle, and he provides me with further help.

--- The thieves are caught, condemned, and led...

<meta>This page continues the philosophical investigation into moral sentiments, transitioning from the discussion of empathy for a criminal benefactor on page 209 to a broader analysis of social gratitude, hypocrisy during a benefactor's fall from grace, and the psychological origins of why we feel affection for those we help.</meta>

<page-num>210</page-num>

THE MANUAL

Among those enlightened by the principles of harmony <note>original: "harmonie"; in this philosophical context, it refers to a state of social and moral balance or a specific system of natural laws governing human interaction.</note>, there are few who are justified in complaining of treachery and ingratitude. While it is true that the "magic" of our laws generally leads us to blame ungrateful conduct—even though we know such behavior is necessary to the goals of the ambitious—this same magic causes some to make an ostentatious show of remaining attached to a benefactor who has fallen into disgrace. Others choose this very moment to effect a superficial reconciliation with their enemy; however, when the disgraced man is a wicked <note>original: "vicieux"; meaning someone characterized by vice or moral corruption.</note> person, these hypocrites do not deceive enlightened people of integrity.

Let us also provide the reason for the affection inspired in us by the person we have helped <note>original: "celui que nous avons obligé"; literally "the one to whom we have done a favor," thereby placing them under a moral obligation.</note>. Many believe this is because we ought to count on someone who has already received a deposit <note>original: "arrhes"; a financial term for earnest money or a down payment, used here metaphorically to describe initial acts of kindness.</note> of what we are disposed to do for them, and that we expect a deserved return. But if the distance that often exists between the protector and the protégé did not prove that such a calculation, at least, cannot always exist—even though the effect is always the same—given the constancy and universality of this effect, one should have assumed a deeper cause for it.

<vocab>harmony, treachery, ingratitude, laws, the ambitious, benefactor, disgrace, hypocrites, protector, protégé, deposit</vocab>

Where does this come from? It arises from self-love <note>original: "l'amour de nous-mêmes"; in 18th-century philosophy, this usually refers to a natural, healthy drive for self-preservation that, in social creatures, naturally expands to include the well-being of others.</note> acting within the laws of a creature destined to live in society; a love which can only create our happiness by spreading itself outward, and which procures for us a sense of well-being and draws to us enjoyments proportioned to its harmonic extension <note>original: "extension harmonique"; the expansion of one's empathy and social bonds to create a wider state of balance or "harmony" with the world.</note>; but which drags us from one precipice to another, stifles us with unease, and drowns us in sorrows when it is not enlightened by the principles of the harmonic point <note>original: "point harmonieux"; the ideal state of moral and social equilibrium where individual needs and social duties are perfectly aligned.</note>, and when we keep it for ourselves alone. And returning to the opening subject of this paragraph, I will conclude it with this—

observation, that the corrupt man, just as much as the man of virtue, feels a pleasure at the sight and the memory of his beneficiary <note>original: "obligé"; the person to whom one has done a favor or a kindness.</note>, whereas on the contrary the wayward man <note>original: "dévoyé"; one who has strayed from the path of morality or "the way."</note> feels a pang at the sight of one to whom he is indebted for a favor, because in that moment he feels that eternal justice and the laws of his being impose upon him an expenditure of love and of sacrifices which might as much alarm his discordant affections <note>original: "discordantes affections"; feelings that are out of tune with the natural, moral order of the universe.</note> as the proposal to commit a crime would distress the man of virtue, should his benefactor come to demand it of his gratitude. ...to lead [someone] to believe that we love or that we esteem their person, to fuel their pride, to nourish their errors—is this not to sin grievously against <term>social harmony</term> <note>original: "harmonie sociale"; in this context, it refers to the spiritual and moral equilibrium that should exist between all beings in a divinely ordered society.</note>? This path, then, could never lead to happiness.

### LXXXVII.

WHEN some adversity <note>original: "contrariété"; meaning setbacks or opposition that test one's resolve.</note> comes to shake your weakness, look toward your **Orient** <note>original: "orient"; in 18th-century mystical and Masonic traditions, the "East" or "Orient" symbolizes the source of divine light, spiritual truth, and the origin of the soul.</note>; it is there that those solicitous faculties, tokens of a necessary bliss, will receive the new senses for the development of your being. It is there that you shall enter into the enjoyment of those ineffable goods for which your connection to them makes you sigh—the seeds of which disturb and painfully agitate those who misplace their laws. Oh, how the thought of death contains a healing balm! How the idea of eternity strips the world of its deceptive luster, and protects us from its pitfalls and its intoxication!

### LXXXVIII.

HOW could men ever discover the truth and the path to happiness! They never trace back to the source of their sorrows, and instead always blame the immediate cause. ### LXXXIX.

**LITERARY ambition** attracts no fewer sorrows than any other; whether you place your hope in your pen or in your sword, it is the same error; every object reacts relatively. The **common people** <note>original: "le vulgaire"; in this context, it refers to the general public or the unlearned masses who judge only by appearances.</note> will envy you; but you, you experience what precedes, what accompanies, and what follows success—and what remains of it at the bottom of the heart after having obtained it in a mixed state, as always happens.

### X C V I I.

WE ARE taught that we only sin because Adam sinned; but how could Adam have sinned if he had not been born with the same necessity to seek happiness which causes us to sin today? ### XCVIII.

SEVERAL sages have given radical truths <note>original: "vérités radicales." In this context, "radical" refers to "root" or fundamental truths rather than extremist views.</note>; but sophists and deceivers have interpreted them so cruelly, and surrounded them with so many absurdities, that they have turned them into doctrines revolting to reason and feeling.

When, therefore, some unfortunate person today desires to enlighten himself, rebuffed on all sides, he soon returns to the pasture <note>original: "la pâture." A metaphor for the common, unthinking way of life or the "fodder" of popular opinion.</note>; for, should he wish to speak of what he feels, where will he find his counterpart? # CONCERNING XEFOLIUS #
<sig>P 3</sig>

<margin>
:
.
</margin>
How, with so much **wit** and so much **character**, are men weak enough to govern themselves by a machine that turns with every wind? <note>The author uses the metaphor of a weather vane ("a machine that turns with every wind") to describe the fickle and changing nature of public opinion.</note> How do these men, usually so proud, so basely solicit the attention of others? How do these commanding minds submit their reason and their experience here to the <term>ostracism</term> <note>original: "ostracisme." Historically referring to the Athenian practice of temporary banishment by popular vote; here, it refers to the social pressure or judgment of the masses.</note> of the people? How do these men, eager for happiness, not dare to follow the opinions and devote themselves to the way of life they truly approve of when they consult only their internal assent?

How? It is because these inconsistencies are the consequences that flow from the magical action and reaction we have upon one another—whose singularly extensive effects vary infinitely, according to the habitual dispositions in which we respectively find ourselves. Now, what misleads the <term>people of fashion</term> <note>original: "gens du bon ton." This refers to the high-society elite who set social standards and trends.</note> in this century of confusion is that indefinite thing that everyone places at the head of their other pretensions to facilitate their success; that thing so little deserved in our day, yet which everyone dares to claim, and which is obtained only by force of impudence; that thing—finally, *social standing*—of which modesty alone seems unworthy. <note>original: "considération." In the 18th century, this term specifically meant the respect or status granted by society, which the author argues is now won through arrogance rather than merit.</note>

<vocab>wit, character, ostracism, inconsistencies, social standing, modesty</vocab>

Let me then explain to you what you have all experienced. Listen: This inner light which sometimes comes to cast a ray...

### C I.
<note>Roman numeral 101.</note>

**NECESSITATED** by the law of the infinite to desire and to hope without ceasing, an abundance of fleeting objects makes man more foolish, more inconstant, more ungrateful, and more unhappy than he would be in a state of want, when he is not imbued with the principles of harmony and grounded in his orient. <note>As noted on the previous page, the "orient" represents the spiritual East, the divine source from which the soul originates and toward which it should be directed.</note> Therefore, desire and hope infinitely only for the infinity of your nature and destiny, because it is only in the way of salvation that we find rest, and that we truly enjoy the accessories.

### C II.
<note>Roman numeral 102.</note>

**The** philosopher who argued that man was necessitated to desire the esteem of his fellows stopped at the surface <note>original: "l’écorce," literally "the bark" or "the shell." A common metaphor in mystical literature for superficial knowledge that does not reach the inner "pith" or truth.</note> of a truth, at a simple effect, as ordinarily happens.

<meta>This page continues the discourse from the previous page regarding the philosopher's view on human necessity. While page 232 ended mid-sentence discussing the "esteem" of others, this page clarifies that the pursuit of happiness is the primary driver of human action, governing how one views praise, slander, and public reputation.</meta>

<page-num>233</page-num>
<header>-> OF XEFOLIUS <-</header>
<note>Xefolius: Likely a character name or a pseudonym used within the text to personify a specific philosophical viewpoint or a "disciple of wisdom."</note>

The only moral necessity of man is to seek his happiness. He who firmly believes he can find it only by devoting himself to contempt <note>original: "mépris." In this context, it refers to the state of being scorned or looked down upon by society.</note> will devote himself to it without difficulty. There is no century that does not provide examples of this, even famous ones. The wayward do not love praise because it is praise, and they do not hate calumnies <note>original: "calomnies." False and malicious statements intended to damage someone's reputation; slander.</note> because they are calumnies; they love praise for what it promises them, and hate calumnies because they threaten them. How indulgent they would be toward the calumny that led them to their goal! What lovely slander that would be which procured for them the object of their desires!

But public esteem being usually favorable to the ambition of each, every person desires it as a means to an end. Add to that the sentiment of our laws—a sentiment that we all have, a sentiment which tells us, more or less distinctly, that happiness comes from harmony, that nothing is estimable except harmonious qualities—and you will find the reason why even the most depraved man is eager <note>original: "jaloux." In 18th-century French, this often meant being "zealously anxious" or "eager" to preserve or achieve something, rather than just "envious."</note> to justify his conduct, and to clothe everything, even his crimes themselves, with harmonious motifs. <note>The author uses "harmony" as a technical term for the spiritual and moral order. Even a criminal tries to frame their actions as being in "harmony" with some perceived logic or higher purpose to justify them to themselves and others.</note>

| :--- | :--- |
| **Moral necessity** | The innate drive for happiness. |
| **Happiness** | The ultimate goal of human action. |
| **Contempt** | Social scorn, which some may accept to reach their goals. |
| **Praise** | Valued only for the benefits it brings. |
| **Calumnies** | Hated only for the obstacles they create. |
| **Public esteem** | A tool used to achieve personal ambition. |
| **Laws** | The internal sense of order and justice. |
| **Harmony** | The source of happiness and the standard of value. |
| **Harmonious motifs** | The justifications used to make actions seem right. |

<meta>This page continues the philosophical inquiry into human motivation. While the previous page discussed the "law of the infinite" and the choice between spiritual essence and external "accessories," this page focuses on the psychological drive for "happiness" and how even the most wayward individuals use the concept of "harmony" to justify their actions.</meta>

<page-num>233</page-num>
<header>ON THE SPIRIT.</header>

Man’s only moral necessity <note>The author argues here that the fundamental "law" governing human behavior is not an abstract code of ethics, but the inescapable drive toward self-interest and fulfillment.</note> is to seek his happiness. He who firmly believes he can find it only by devoting himself to contempt <note>original: "au mépris." This refers to someone who chooses a path that society finds shameful because they believe it will bring them ultimate satisfaction or power.</note> will devote himself to it without difficulty. There is no century that does not provide examples of this, even famous ones. The wayward <note>original: "Les dévoyés." Those who have strayed from the path of conventional virtue.</note> do not love praise because it is praise, and they do not hate slanders because they are slanders; they love praise for what it promises them, and they hate slanders because they threaten them. How indulgent they would be toward the slander that led them to their goal! What "amiable" gossip that would be, which procured for them the object of their desires!

But since public esteem is usually favorable to each person’s ambition, everyone desires it as a means to an end. Add to this our feeling for the laws <note>original: "sentiment de nos loix." This implies an innate moral compass or an internalized social contract.</note>, a feeling that we all possess, a feeling which tells us, more or less distinctly, that happiness comes from harmony, and that nothing is estimable except harmonious qualities; and you will find the reason why even the most depraved man is eager to justify his conduct, and to clothe everything—even his very crimes—in harmonious motives <note>The author observes a deep human psychological trait: the need to frame even one's worst actions as being in "harmony" with some greater good or personal logic, in order to maintain a sense of internal peace or social justification.</note>.

**Key Vocabulary:**
*   **Moral necessity:** The driving force of human action.
*   **Happiness:** The ultimate goal of every individual.
*   **Slander:** False statements intended to damage a reputation.
*   **Praise:** Commendation or public approval.
*   **Public esteem:** The value placed on an individual by society.
*   **Ambition:** The desire for rank or power.
*   **Laws:** The rules (either natural or social) governing behavior.
*   **Harmony:** The state of alignment with truth or the natural order.

<summary>This page argues that the pursuit of happiness is the primary driver of human behavior and explains how people seek public esteem primarily as a tool for their own ambition. It concludes that even the most corrupt individuals feel a psychological need to justify their crimes as being "harmonious" or morally consistent.</summary>

<keywords>happiness, moral necessity, public esteem, self-justification, harmony, psychology of crime, ambition</keywords>

--- Page 261 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 233 regarding the rationalizations humans use to justify their behavior. While the previous page touched on the pursuit of happiness and the internal "sentiment of the laws," this page explores how individuals use external circumstances to excuse moral failures.</meta>

<page-num>234</page-num>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<header>THE HANDBOOK</header>

When his wrongdoings become too evident, he will have recourse to the "present times"—to those embarrassing circumstances from which he had to extricate himself; to the cruel alternative of failing his goal and losing the fruit of so much labor, or of obeying necessity—in short, to the "reason of things" <note>original: "raison des choses." This refers to a situational logic or rationalization where one argues that the specific "facts" of a situation override general moral laws.</note>. This is a captivating, deceptive, and discordant reason; it is the reason by which the conqueror and the highwayman justify their murders, just as the *amiable man* <note>original: "l’homme aimable." In the 18th century, this was a social archetype of a charming, socially successful person. The author is being critical here, suggesting that polished social graces often mask a lack of true virtue.</note> justifies his ingratitude, his trickery, his baseness, and his betrayals. And since this displacement of laws is universal, differing only in its various objects, and since everyone, upon self-examination, feels more or less guilty, the authority of this "reason of things" gains credit in a society as that society moves further away from nature. It gives an appearance of wisdom to that very fashionable *maxim* <note>original: "dictum."</note>: that one must simply do as everyone else does, if one does not wish to be both a fool and despised. Because of this, how many people examine the morality they adopt less than the fabric they wear! For them, custom is the infallible arbiter of both principles and taste.

<meta>This page transitions from the social critiques of the previous page to a discussion on the spiritual and moral implications of beauty and attraction. It continues the author's exploration of "internal laws" versus external appearances.</meta>

<page-num>235</page-num>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<header>OF XÉFOLIUS.</header>

### C I I I.

Observe how this *physiognomy* <note>original: "physionomie." In the 18th century, physiognomy was the study of facial features as a direct reflection of a person's inner character or soul.</note>, which expresses at once candor, tenderness, amiable kindness, warmth, and strength of soul, moves every heart, while bold or lascivious beauty barely stirs the senses of a few depraved men!

Therefore, to know the state of the **laws of your being**, examine what kind of beauty attracts you. If it is that of the nature of destiny—if it is the burning expression of **harmonious laws** that inflames you, to the point of recognizing and adoring the **Divinity** within it—then you will know the true meaning of love. You will give thanks to heaven for having caused you to be born for that **eternal felicity** <note>original: "éternelle félicité," meaning supreme happiness or blessedness.</note> toward which you may travel by a path strewn with flowers. But if you are attracted only by the charms of transient nature, or by the lure of a triumph envied by your peers, you will be inconstant, wicked, and unhappy.

Such are the results of our dispositions and of *relatives* <note>original: "relatifs." In this philosophical context, it refers to the relationships or correlations between an individual's internal state and the external objects they are drawn to.</note>. If the author of the **most famous novel** <note>This is likely a reference to Jean-Jacques Rousseau and his best-selling novel *Julie, or the New Heloise* (1761), which the author seems to be criticizing for its psychological inconsistencies.</note> had known the nature of our being, he would not have given his hero virtues that are incompatible with the displacement of his laws, and especially not with the displacement of his **law of the infinite**. A man

<meta>catchword: man</meta>

<summary>The author argues that true love is found by being attracted to moral and divine beauty rather than fleeting physical charms. He criticizes popular novelists for creating characters whose virtues contradict the fundamental laws of human nature.</summary>

generous, sensitive regarding the <term>point of honor</term> <note>original: "point d’honneur." Truthfulness, refinement, bravery, generosity—all virtues and all noble qualities share this common source and are linked in the same chain, wherever the sense of beauty forms the foundation of one's character. I will add that the frosts of age cannot guarantee protection from this love which old age can no longer inspire; but for that very reason, the more an old man is sensitive to the **supremely beautiful** <note>original: "le beau par excellence"</note> and the more he knows it, the less he will demand of the beloved object, because he knows that the <term>Great Architect</term> <note>A common Enlightenment and Masonic term for God, portraying the Creator as a master builder or designer of the universe.</note>, who does nothing in vain, has limited the <term>sympathetic power</term> <note>In 18th-century philosophy, "sympathy" referred to a natural attraction or resonance between two beings or things.</note> of one of our two natures to a specific span of time; and that love between youth and decrepitude is a sentiment composed of discords, a complete depravity in one who expects sensual pleasures in exchange for disgust.

<header>ABOUT XEFOLIUS.</header>

...yield according to the circumstances, but in accordance with customary forms.

However, when, being sensitive to harmony, your spirit leads you to covet status <term>considération: social standing or the esteem of others</term>, and to choose your friends and your company based on the opinion of the masses, can you really complain about what you experience?

Nature does not conform to our vain institutions or our proud conventions. The great Principle <note>A common Enlightenment and Esoteric term for the Divine or the fundamental source of all being.</note> recognizes no greatness other than that which brings us closer to Him. Judge and choose, therefore, with a sense of harmony, and you will taste the sweetness of the action and reaction of our laws.

What makes the inferior so agreeable to his superior? It is complaisance, truthfulness, gentleness, friendship, and the selfless devotion of the courtier—in short, the hypocrisy of everything a man *ought* to be toward all his fellow beings. Thus, he dons this character—which nature suggests to him—wherever he wishes to please or to succeed.
