Like discordant instruments, the pitch <term>diapason: the standard pitch used to tune an orchestra; here used as a metaphor for a moral or social standard</term> of one does not suit the other, and the pitch of truth is rejected by everyone, because it necessarily clashes with all the various interests and prejudices of those who have lost their way <note>original: "dévoyés" — those who have strayed from the path of reason, virtue, or natural law</note>.

The merit of each person is to play their own part well. But where is this ever seen? It is certainly not in those social circles where the accident of birth, which distributes the seats there, persuades those of the highest rank that they hold the same standing in the empire of the mind <note>original: "l'empire de l'esprit" — the realm of intellect, wit, or spirit</note>, and that they have the right to command even the thoughts of those who find themselves beneath them.

### C V I I I.

A man rooted in discord easily gains an apparent superiority over a gentle man, who does not cling to such frivolities; who sacrifices many secondary matters for the love of peace; and who ensures that, if dominating sometimes provides a moment of pleasure, in a hundred other circumstances, this discordant passion—whose weak origin he recognizes—finds itself humbled.

If we defer to someone whom we despise and hate, it is only because they are repugnant to us, or because they are feared; and they are only repugnant or feared because, like a <term>frenzied man</term><note>original: "frénétique" — referring to someone in a state of agitated delirium or uncontrolled passion</note>, the intensity of his inner malaise gives him a violent willpower, and his repulsive <term>emanations</term><note>In the author's mystical framework, "emanations" are the spiritual or energetic atmospheres that a person radiates based on their internal moral state</note> carry the malice of the errors that torment him.

### CX.

NOTHING proves more clearly how impossible it is to root out our inner resonance with the laws of harmony than the hatred the wicked man feels toward the person he knows he has unjustly offended, or toward the man of virtue who has had the misfortune of catching him <term>in the act</term> <note>original: "en flagrant délit" — red-handed or in the middle of a wrongful deed.</note>. ### CXII.

ALTHOUGH you may not have reached the height of intimacy with those you habitually frequent, they have acquired rights of preference over you. This truth, felt by all, can only be obscured by the subtleties of that spirit which muddles everything.

Do not wait, then, for them to speak to you of their needs; if you can help them, go to them; this is the way to aid them in more than one sense. This is because actions possess an eloquence that speech cannot approach; because in all things we desire a sense of <term>wholeness</term> <note>original: "ensemble" — a philosophical term for the harmonious unity of intent and action.</note>, and because the ascendancy of virtue carries away all hearts where its seed still pulses.

### CXIII.

NOTHING is good for man except that which is part of <term>harmony</term> <note>In this context, "harmony" refers to the Stoic and Enlightenment ideal of living in accordance with the natural order of the universe.</note>; this is why despair increases our pains, while resignation softens them; and it is also why we despise useless lamentations, while we admire a gentle silence.

There are many similar truths that are misunderstood or refuted when one possesses more wit than feeling, or more vanity than wit, or when one is ignorant of the nature of the <term>social being</term> <note>The "social being" (être social) refers to the idea that humans are naturally designed for community and mutual obligation.</note>, and the **harmonious point**—that rallying point for all truths necessary to man. It is, for example, certain that with reasoning detached from feeling, one could easily prove that our affliction should always be proportional to the magnitude of evils and to the number of individuals who suffer. But if that were possible, what would become of man, when one considers what is happening across the globe?

The pity inspired in me by an unhappy neighbor moves me to help him; but of what use would my alarms and my grief be to the distant inhabitants of a desolate land, given my inability to help them, if it were not...

### CXV.

THE hypocritical and erasing duties <note>French: *oblitérans*. The author refers to social or political obligations that force an individual to suppress their true character or moral principles.</note> that one is sometimes obliged to render to those who rule are a torment for the man sensitive to his laws; and it is because we are made to obey laws, and not the men who hate them, that one only finds true men in places where only the laws are obeyed.

To say that man is born with a love of **independence** is, again, to mistake the effect for the cause; for, just as the individual is unjust when society is just, society would reject individual and unlimited **liberty**, even though the individual constantly tends toward it. The individual wants an unlimited liberty so that nothing can ever oppose his **happiness**; but he only wants to be free because he wants to be happy, and he only wants to be happy because he is necessitated to want it, and he is only necessitated to want it because he must become so. ->DE XÉFOLIUS.<-
R 2

[happi]ness in a perfect independence, who would make himself the most unhappy of men if he were not restrained by the laws and by the fear from which he would wish to free himself. Order being the true element of man, the greatest service to render him is to maintain him within it by harmonious means; and these means reside no more in goodness and generosity than they do in exactitude and severity; rather, they are found in the mixture created by love and justice in accord. Finally, this unlimited liberty that the individual might desire would be rejected by society, which sees in it an anarchy more fertile in desolations than any despotism.

### CXVI.

NOW that you know that every sin is punished, learn also that it is a sin to fail in the proprieties <note>French: *convenances*. This refers to the moral and social obligations or "fitness" required to live harmoniously within a community.</note> of the great society; and that by neglecting what would be advantageous to it, you will lose far more than you hoped to gain.

### CXV.

->CXXI.<-

BECAUSE we do not know the past life of a person who suffers; because we are ignorant of what they have to repair, to lose, or to gain; what the affections are that lead them away from harmony (which we often ignore in ourselves as perfectly as in others), there are situations where illnesses, poverty, persecution, and inner turmoil form a group of miseries so frightening that, in our ignorance of the nature of our being, we cannot reconcile such evils with the goodness and justice of the **Almighty** <term>Tout-puissant: God, or the all-powerful creator.</term>. How the tears of this anticipation differ from those shed upon the tomb of some corrupt interest <note>Refers to mourning motivated by the loss of material inheritance or social status rather than genuine love.</note>, as happens far more often than tears shed upon the ashes of the one who is missed!

O you who have made me know what is sweetest and what is most bitter, in presenting to you the fruit of my sighs, I pay you homage to eternal wisdom! Ah! May death, which reunites kindred spirits, have charms for you on the last day of your life! Far from its murderous scythe cutting the bonds that unite us then, it will only increase the delight of their embrace. Freed from earthly shackles, our souls—wholly devoted to what they adore—merging into one another, will exist henceforth only for felicity <term>felicity: A state of intense happiness or blessedness, often used in a spiritual context to describe the joy of the afterlife.</term>.

### 123. <note>original: C X X I I I</note>

You only wish to succeed, you only wish to make a fortune because you have a son; this means, if I am not mistaken, that if you did not have this son, you would be a philosopher <note>The author suggests that people often use their children as an excuse for their own greed or social climbing, claiming they would live simply if not for their dependents.</note>. But you deceive yourself; your child is only a pretext: you follow your own obsession or your error, just as the miser and the spendthrift do, and those proud, ambitious men who still claim the admiration, and almost the gratitude, of the weak whom they devastate.

If your son has needs beyond what is necessary, let him procure for himself the means to satisfy them, and let him disabuse himself of his illusions. If he wishes to be a greater lord than his father, let him learn how one rises; but that is not what you ought to teach him. Phocion <note>Phocion (c. 402 – c. 318 BC) was an Athenian statesman and general, celebrated for his extreme honesty and his refusal to change his simple lifestyle despite his high rank.</note>, who was certainly your equal, replied to those who pressed him on this matter:
> "If my children are wise, they
> will have enough, just as I do; if they
> are foolish and debauched, I do not want
> to leave them the means to maintain their
> depravity <term>depravity: The original French "turpitude" refers to base, shameful, or wicked behavior.</term>."

...avoid fools, yield to those who are wrong, and lead by example. <note>This continues the sentence from the previous page: "...the wise man must... yield to all those you meet."</note>

If you travel on horseback, do not disturb pedestrians, because you must practice justice on every occasion; it is by neglecting its smallest precepts that young people become wicked men.

One who, in his rapid course, forces his coachman to strike everything that hinders him, will unscrupulously crush all his friends to reach his goal.

The value of good works lies not in the worth assigned to them by common opinion, but in the sentiment that inspires them. By turning away from a pleasure to prevent harm to a stranger, or to set a traveler back on his path, it is possible that you perform a more harmonious <note>In this context, "harmonious" refers to an act that aligns the individual soul with the divine order of the universe.</note> act than a great almsgiving would be in other circumstances.

### 128 <note>original: CXXVIII</note>

BY examining men according to the infallible principles of the system of harmony, <note>In this school of thought, "harmony" refers to the alignment of the human soul with the divine order.</note> one sees few to whom misery does not give profound lessons, and who are sufficiently modified <note>original: "modifiés"; a technical term in this philosophy referring to the spiritual refinement or "shaping" of the soul through experience.</note> to be able to bear a greater ease, and even less prosperity. If one who groans at his mediocrity, and attributes to it the evils brought upon him by the errors of his own spirit, were to achieve his ends, he would only become more foolish, more wicked, more unhappy, and further delayed in his modification.

The situation in which we currently find ourselves will always be profitable for the future when we bear it with resignation, when we know how to gather its fruit, and when, by new infractions, <note>Refers to spiritual or moral transgressions against one's conscience.</note> we do not necessitate new adversities.

All harmonies are interconnected, and he who is not in accord with himself receives wounds from everywhere.

<vocab>harmony, poverty, patience, Potosí, misery, prosperity, resignation, modification</vocab>

### 129 <note>original: C X X I X.</note>

TO SAY that we must stifle the passions (in the sense that teachers give to this precept), because they lead us astray, is as foolish as if we were invited to burn our palate to cure us of gluttony, or to cut off our tongue and hands to make us unable to slander and murder; for neither of our two natures <note>Commonly refers to the dual nature of man: the physical/material and the spiritual/divine.</note> has received anything useless: what we call passion is only an abuse or a displacement of our laws. The Peripatetics <note>Followers of Aristotle, who taught that virtue lies in the moderation of passions (the "golden mean") rather than their total suppression.</note> saw this more clearly than the Stoics, who wanted the wise man to be unreachable by emotions, unalterable, and imperturbable.

The immortal Being has received, within the law of the infinite, an unquenchable principle of activity. Destined for boundless bliss, the desire, fear, and hope that stir him push him toward whatever promises him emotions; but when the great, the princes, the kings, whose every wish is anticipated, see only burdens in their duties; when they seek the emotions of desire, fear, and hope from the hounds of their pack and the stags of their forests; when they neglect both those

### 132 <note>original: CXXXII.</note>

Just as one takes care to hide what is contemptible, ambitious mediocrity wraps itself in arrogance <note>original: *morgue*; in this context, it refers to a haughty or disdainful attitude used to mask a lack of merit</note>; and to gain height and stature <note>original: *embonpoint*; literally "plumpness," but used here metaphorically to describe a bloated sense of social importance</note>, it props itself up, stuffs itself with titles, and surrounds itself with the names of its ancestors. But whoever is intrinsically worthy is happy to show themselves openly: they are not anxious about their reputation; and if they have penetrated deeper into the truth, they will not even think of their status, because they know that the child of vanity is the enemy of peace. Vanity stifles the gratitude that our kindnesses might deserve; it alienates the hearts of others so thoroughly that even our closest companions conspire against our advancement. They can only find joy in the events that humble us; and in this malicious pleasure, there are no other feelings at play than those inspired by our own ill-founded pretensions, which aim only for the satisfaction of forcing our equals to pay us homage.

<vocab>harmony, mediocrity, arrogance, stature, intrinsically, truth, vanity, gratitude, kindnesses, feelings, pretensions</vocab>

### 133 <note>original: CXXXIII.</note>

The philosophy that provides us with constant satisfaction only produces this effect because it is harmonious and conforms to our nature. It is not because I think this way that I feel well, but rather because I *ought* to think this way, and only by thinking this way can I be happy. This distinction is subtle, yet it is essential.

An error may please me; but in yielding to it, I would not be long in being afflicted by its results and the consequences that flow from it; for as soon as I displace one of my <term>laws</term> <note>In this philosophical context, "laws" refer to the inherent moral and spiritual principles that govern human nature and its connection to the divine</note>, the law of fear necessarily follows it. Because the law of fear was given to us to watch over our preservation and our happiness, it cannot lose sight of even the smallest things upon which we make our existence and our happiness depend, so that the moment one of these things is threatened, fear calls our faculties to its aid.

When, therefore, hope has for its object only a <term>negative happiness</term> <note>A "negative happiness" refers to a state where one seeks merely the absence of pain or trouble, rather than the active presence of spiritual fulfillment or virtue</note>, desire and fear are confounded together, and the unease <note>original: *mal-aise*</note> is great; but when it has some prosperity 

<vocab>philosophy, nature, happiness, fear, preservation, existence, hope, unease, prosperity</vocab>

as its object, the emotions of desire and fear are either soothing or unsettling, according to the promises or threats within the interplay of universal laws.

But as the fermentation of the seeds of discord from our errors already causes <term>internal tearing</term> <note>original: *déchiremens*. This refers to the spiritual anguish and fragmentation of the self that occurs when one acts against moral law</note>, and since we cannot sin without sensing the punishment related to our infraction—a punishment necessary for our <term>refinement</term> <note>original: *modification*. In this context, it refers to the transformative process by which a soul is corrected and brought back into its proper state</note>—judge then the internal state of certain straying souls! Judge if any can know a pure pleasure; and admire the marvelous simplicity of the chain of all the laws of nature, which work unceasingly for the development and refinement of beings, and to push man, even through the abuse he makes of his liberty, toward the goal for which he was created!

What has led many philosophers astray is having considered man from only a single perspective, whereas, to truly know him, one must consider him from four perspectives.

By his physical laws, he is linked to the animal kingdom; by his social laws, he is linked to his fellow men; by the laws of his destiny, he is linked to beings of the higher classes; and by his law of the infinite, he is linked to the Divinity.

### C X X X V.

THOSE whose disoriented and wandering mind has difficulty fixing itself within limits would wish that the greatest truths appeared to their eyes as material and sensible <note>In this context, "sensible" means things that can be perceived by the five senses.</note> objects. ### CXXXVI.

SINCE our development is gradual, and we only advance toward the truth step by step; since we are modified only by experience, and no trickery or policy is capable of shielding us from the hammer blows <note>The author uses the metaphor of a blacksmith's "hammer blows" to describe the painful but necessary trials of life.</note> necessary for the malleability <term>ductility: the quality of being easily shaped or molded</term> and shaping of the moral being; how many people would not be terrified by a sentence that condemned them to begin living again in the same uncertainties, in the same ignorance, and under the same conditions they have lived—to pass once more through everything they have experienced since their birth!

Nevertheless, those who have gone astray flatter themselves in vain; by not changing their principles, the days remaining to them will be no more enviable than those of the past; and far more frightening days still await them!

### CXXXVIII.

AMELIA finds in her beauty the source of all her sorrows, and she is not mistaken.

Yet, were you not at once beautiful, poor, and happy in other times? In that solitude, which would make you shudder today—though you regret it when the aftertaste of the happiness you once tasted there, by conforming to the laws of your being, recalls it to your heart—in that solitude, I say, you shed tears! But answer: what made you shed them, and what dried them?

Those who breathed the spirit of pride into you, AMELIA, were they happy....? And you believed them....! Is it not by changing your orientation <note>original: *orient*; used here as a metaphor for one's moral compass or the direction of one's life</note> that you saw your happiness decline?

Luxury has thrown you into slavery; prejudice <term>prejudice: in this context, the social conventions and "worldly" expectations of high society</term> holds you there; and your boredom and your heartbreaks stem only from the lack of will and the indecision of your disoriented mind.

### C X L.

**IT IS** a cruel miscalculation to wish to profit from society without returning anything to it; yet this is what almost all men strive for, so to speak, at least in their desires: but idleness is punished by feelings of disgust, and by the boredom that the useful man never knows; and this restless independence, as much as discordant ambition, attracts countless accidents and distressing events, specifically through the very efforts one makes to find and assemble a happiness that is opposed by both the laws of our being and the laws of social harmony. <term>social harmony: the 18th-century concept that a well-ordered society should mirror the divine order of the universe, where every part functions for the good of the whole.</term>

It is further sufficient for our projects or our occupations to be discordant for pain to take hold, and for them to be harmonious for pleasure to blend with them.

A painter, a laborer, a carpenter, etc. <note>The author implies that any honest work, when done in accordance with "harmony," provides a spiritual satisfaction that idleness cannot.</note>

I mean by this, that two things are indispensably necessary for the happiness of man: an occupation and a harmonious heart. Such is the order of heaven; in vain would one seek to evade it. Given the manner in which the Grand Architect <note>The "Grand Architect" is a common Enlightenment-era and Masonic term for God, emphasizing the divine as a designer of a rational, law-governed universe.</note> has combined the nature of our being, punishment is found at the end of disobedience; but the frequent infractions of which man makes himself guilty cause the state of unease <note>original: "mal-aise." Someone will answer me to this: "With what can I occupy myself in the rank and circumstances where I am placed? I have neither talent nor trade beneath my birth, and what I know how to do is of use neither in the places nor the times where I live; no one makes use of my deep political maneuvering, nor my ease in scolding other men, nor my art for intrigue and seduction; nor my superficial charm, nor my talent for mockery; nor my moral indifference—in short, of nothing of..."

### CXLI.

If an ambitious man, whose plan is known, yields to pity or to some other law of his being <note>The "law of his being" refers to innate human nature or conscience, which the author suggests is inescapable even for the most hardened individuals.</note> in a circumstance where his goals would have required him to violate it, the practitioners of discord will accuse him of lacking strength, and he himself will be wretched enough to reproach himself for it. Yet there is no great man—be he a conqueror or a highwayman, a member of the court or a regular at the Place de Grève <note>original: "la Grêve." The Place de Grève in Paris was the public square where executions were carried out. The author is bitingly suggesting that there is no moral difference between "great" men of the royal court and the criminals who end up on the gallows.</note>—who does not have some weakness of this kind to reproach himself for. The history of tyrants does not present a single one who has not occasionally yielded to the cry of the laws of his being, even if only to catch his breath.

The man who is generally hated is, without a doubt, a loathsome man. These rare individuals are felt more easily than they can be defined: harmful emanations burst forth from their ferocious personality and form around them an atmosphere that attracts the hatred of those...

This mistake is made by those who, for lack of knowing both the nature of our destiny and the nature of things, adore an almighty god in the dark idol of discord, and are all the more disposed toward devotion...

### C X L I I I.

FOR the good and just man, when he wishes ill upon the wicked, it is out of a feeling of benevolence—one very hidden in truth, and often unknown even to himself. But in searching the depths of his heart, he will find that this wish stems: 1st, from his love for his fellow men, whom this wicked person persecutes; 2nd, from his reminiscences <note>original: *réminiscences*. In this philosophical context, it refers to the spiritual recollection of innate truths or past lessons learned by the soul.</note> which have taught him that suffering and adversity enlighten, correct, and improve man, and consequently make him hope that this wicked person will amend his ways through misfortune. Thus, if this just and good man is not enlightened by the principles of harmony <term>harmony (l’harmonie): a core concept in this philosophy referring to the divine order and the proper alignment of the soul with the laws of the universe</term>, it may happen that he allows himself to be led, by his very virtue, toward some forbidden action, for which he will certainly be punished even in this world.

### C X L V.

A reference to Gaius Maecenas, the Roman patron of Virgil and Horace; here it means a wealthy supporter of the arts.</note> of artists; and yet Lisias is unhappy because of the obsession that favor brings him, because of the sacrifices it demands of his sensitivity and his pride, because of the resistance encountered by an influence <term>influence (crédit): in the 18th century, "credit" referred to one's political weight and social standing at court</term> that seeks no limits, because of the disorder of his affairs, caused by the vain remedies applied to jaded and weary faculties, by the jealousy that pierces him at the slightest distraction, at the lightest preference he thinks he perceives for another, and by so many daily contradictions that agitate him in so many opposing directions, that his pains sometimes reach the point of rage.

### C X L V I I I.

HOW can there be people who dare to maintain that love is a purely physical passion? Has anyone ever seen a person become so impassioned as to lose sleep and health over a dish <note>The author uses a culinary metaphor here to compare physical appetite for food with the much deeper, obsessive nature of romantic love.</note> that can be found everywhere? But we disdain a beautiful courtesan to make ourselves the slave of a less attractive woman, because the courtesan—indifferent and greedy—presents a great deal of discordance; whereas the virtuous woman <note>original: *femme honnête*. In this period, this refers to a woman of social standing who possesses moral integrity and refined manners.</note> promises us harmonious happiness. We say to ourselves: *I shall love, and I shall be loved;* and it is only by making us hope for this great good—whether through the expression of her features <note>original: *physionomie*. Referring to the contemporary belief that a person's character and soul could be read through their facial expressions.</note> or the maneuvering of her wit—that the courtesan triumphs over her rival. The examples one might offer in opposition belong either to luxury, or to depravity—which wishes to taste everything—or perhaps to those men who possess no depth of character <note>original: *rien de prononcé*. Literally "nothing pronounced," meaning men without strong moral convictions or distinct personality.</note> and who, after having seduced, tyrannize, exhibit <term>exhibit (affichent): to ostentatiously display a conquest or a lover in public for the sake of vanity or social status</term>, and debase the object of their alleged love, tearing apart a tender heart as if to punish it for having prostituted itself through sacrifices of which they confess themselves unworthy, by the very acts they perform to display their triumph.

<vocab>love, passion, courtesan, virtuous woman, physiognomy, maneuvering of the wit, depravity, triumph</vocab>

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;But, as the love which can disturb a harmony is a forbidden fruit, the great Architect <note>original: *Architecte*. This is so true that the art of the coquette consists in maintaining her admirers between these two extremes; that beauty which gives no hope receives only cold tributes and has no slave; that the most beautiful princess is only a beautiful statue for her lackeys; and that the woman who, after the final sacrifice <note>original: *le dernier sacrifice*. ### 150.

WHEN fortune is set against us, we blame the living and the dead, and beings who are unfeeling and without power; we often push madness so far as to blaspheme against divine justice, and we stubbornly refuse to seek within ourselves the cause of our sufferings. We do this because such a discovery would force us to diminish the high opinion we have of our own skill, as well as those external things through which we assert our value; it would also alter both our grand hopes and the illusion of our superiority.

### CLII.

**Politics** teaches that, to have **peace**, one must be ready for **war** <note>original: *si vis pacem, para bellum*.</note>; a maxim that seems to apply even more to individuals than to sovereigns, when one considers the number of enemies each has to fear, and the frequent opportunities for strife.

However, by living in accordance with your laws, you will only be exposed to conflict as an agent of **universal laws**. If you then perish, it will be an evil for your murderer and a benefit for you; if, on the contrary, you triumph, you will have been nothing but the executor of an order, and you will have only obeyed the law responsible for ensuring your preservation.

But in no case should the strong overwhelm the weak; danger and necessity alone authorize retaliation. This law, engraved in the hearts of all beings and so vital to the **preservation of species**, is never violated except by man in a state of total **discordance** <term>discordance: a state of being spiritually or morally "out of tune" with the natural and divine order of the universe.</term>; in—

### CLIII.

IT IS commonly believed that **sovereigns** only make **war** to arrive at a happy **peace**, and that men only toil so that they may finally **rest**; but by examining things more closely, we see on the contrary that sovereigns only make peace to gather new resources and wage war with greater advantage; and that men only rest so they may run after their **chimera** <note>In this context, a "chimera" refers to an illusory or unattainable dream of perfect happiness that drives human ambition.</note> with new strength. This is because, being all of the same species, we are all equally necessitated to seek **happiness**. The error through which sovereigns hope <note>original: "l'espèrent." The author suggests that rulers hope to find fulfillment through these cycles of power, just as individuals do in their private lives.</note> is the same as that of **private individuals**; there is only a difference in the objects, the means, and the effects. The actions of sovereigns dazzle us by their scale; those of individuals sometimes seem contemptible to us; but they are all driven equally by the goal toward which they tend.

<vocab>sovereigns, war, peace, happiness, universal law, chimera, private individuals, Reflections</vocab>

### CLIV.

**DEMAND** nothing from anyone; feign not to notice the <term>failings</term> <note>original: "manquemens." This refers to lapses in social etiquette or personal loyalty.</note> toward you. Proper conduct stems from a fine and delicate sensibility, and from an enlightened spirit; such behaviors become distorted as soon as they are made an object of commerce <note>That is, when one expects something in return for being polite or virtuous.</note>, and henceforth yield nothing but briars and thorns. Unstitch, but do not tear away from those from whom you believe you must separate; never complain about them, and do not flatter yourself that you will find relief in the malice of spreading what you might say about them. What has happened to you, far from being a subject of **contention** for you, should on the contrary help you advance in your **harmony**: no one deviates from this principle without repenting of it sooner or later.

### CLV.

**WHEN** an injustice or an act of malice rouses in you a feeling of indignation, before reasoning about this injustice or malice, reason through your own feeling, so that it does not turn into anger. Say to yourself:
> » It is to endure this that I have need of
> » **wisdom**; might this not be an old debt
> » that I am paying? If I allow myself to be shaken, I will not

### OF XEFOLIUS.

-or, with this difference: that the principles of the sage are always the same, but when opinions on honor change, it is proof that honor is declining, that vice is breaking its dams, and that infamy is about to spread in torrents. In this sort of deluge, consideration is given only to what people can do for or against the interest of corruption; the man himself is always what one examines the least; the oppressed weak find no protectors; upon what would he base his complaints? Whom do they not accuse? To what end could he be useful? Why would one expose oneself for him? What motives could engage people persuaded that one no longer believes in either virtue or honor? 

But the man capable of playing his whole game <note>original: "jouer tout son jeu." Likely meaning a person who acts without moral restraint to achieve their goals.</note> is listened to; his conformity warns that he may become either a dangerous enemy or a useful instrument, and that by justifying him, one makes one's own apology. ### ON XEFOLIUS

I will observe on this subject that every maxim of conduct, like every question of morality, is discussed by each person according to the **premise** <note>original: "donnée." In this context, it refers to the starting data point or the fundamental assumption from which an entire line of reasoning begins.</note> from which they start—a premise most often unknown to the opponent, just as the opponent's premise is unknown to them. Since these different premises proceed from the particular idea each person forms of **happiness** <note>original: "bonheur."</note>, of good, and of evil—and these ideas themselves stem from the particular idea each person has of the nature of our being—it follows that these various premises, born from one another and mutually unknown, are in fact nothing more than the different consequences of different **major and minor premises** <term>major and minor premises: These are technical terms from Aristotelian logic. A syllogism (a logical argument) consists of a major premise, a minor premise, and a conclusion. The author is saying that people argue over conclusions without realizing their starting "logic blocks" are different.</term> that are reciprocally unrecognized. Thus, disputes are interminable as long as one has not agreed upon the nature of the being to which everything that divides both hearts and opinions relates.

It would therefore be necessary, before discussing any morality whatsoever, to come to an agreement on the **nature of man**; without this, each person’s conclusions may be perfectly correct relative to the secret premises from which they started—and which were never mentioned—so that, even with the best will and all possible modesty, they could not fail to persist in their

# OF XEFOLIUS. <note>Xéfolius appears to be a name used in a philosophical or pedagogical dialogue, common in 18th-century French literature to represent a specific viewpoint or character.</note>

When Plato said that children and the elderly are the most susceptible to religion, it was because he felt that children, not yet led astray by great errors, are more disposed to yield to the assent of our laws; and that the elderly, enlightened by long experience and disillusioned by the vain promise of fleeting objects, must offer little resistance to the invitations that the laws of our destined nature never cease to offer us. This seems so well-founded to me that I would add that it is not rare to see elderly people whom the fear of not doing enough drives to excesses and ridiculous superstitions.

Always compelled to hope and to seek happiness, does one see many men depraved enough to place their law of the infinite <note>This refers to the inherent human drive toward the eternal or the divine.</note> in the admiration that an impiety should earn them—

### CLXII.

I will not stubbornly pursue the superfluous things that universal laws make vanish before me, but I will struggle against the waves ready to submerge me; I will not spare myself in earning my livelihood; I will aid my neighbor even under the assassin's dagger; I will resist the tyrant who commands me to commit a crime, and I will expose myself to pain and death wherever harmony <note>original: "l’harmonie." In Enlightenment-era philosophy, this refers to the balanced order of the universe or the moral "fitness" of things.</note> requires it.

### CLXIV.

BEFORE falling asleep, review your day; acknowledge the faults you have committed; thank God for the graces He has granted you, and your good spirit <term>bon génie: a protective or guiding spiritual entity, similar to a guardian angel</term> for the services it has rendered you, and your sleep will be calm, and your heart will bloom upon your awakening. Then address your prayer to the Eternal, and invoke your good spirit: foresee what you will have to do during the day you are about to enter, and walk with the full confidence that nothing unfortunate can happen to you.

### CLXV.

JUST AS there are collective spirits <term>esprits de corps: the shared identity, morale, or "atmosphere" of a specific group</term>, spirits of families, and of states, whose true origin is not always easy to discover, so too there are cities, provinces, and entire nations led astray by an object that impresses upon them a distinct spirit and character, and whose emanations and results inspire in foreigners a disgust, an indefinable unease—

THE MANUAL

...indefinable, proportionate to the difference in customs and maxims by which they are accustomed to conduct themselves. The more the dominant object <note>In this context, the "object" refers to a central obsession or shared goal, such as wealth, power, or social status, that consumes a society’s attention.</note> is likely to provoke disputes between the various castes of the **social hierarchy**, the more there will necessarily be a relationship between them; consequently, the higher classes will increasingly resemble the lowest, most base ones. The **nobleman**, in everything relating to this object of discord (and what would not relate to it?)—the **nobleman**, I say, descending into the arena, will leave **justice**, **honor**, and **virtue** to rest alongside the parchments of his ancestors. He will employ cunning and the lowest of means without harming his reputation, because such means are viewed as a kind of weapon common to all, which each person uses according to their skill; the public interest is focused solely on the gain or loss of its favorite object. 

But what a place to dwell for the loving, trusting, and benevolent man! Surrounded by **moral corpses**, his expansive sensitivity, forced back toward its source, gives him the sensation of the **torture of Mezentius** <note>original: "ſupplice de MÉZENCE." In Virgil's *Aeneid*, the tyrant Mezentius punished his victims by binding a living person to a decomposing corpse, chest to chest, until the living died of the infection—a metaphor for a pure soul forced to live among the morally corrupt.</note>; he will be reduced to solitude if he cannot escape without disrupting the harmony.

<summary>The author describes how a society's shared obsession with material or social gain corrupts all classes, forcing even the nobility to abandon honor for cunning and leaving virtuous individuals in a state of agonizing isolation.</summary>
<keywords>social hierarchy, corruption, virtue, Mezentius, moral decay, social discord, solitude</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the conclusion of the philosophical treatise, moving from the corrupting influence of society (the "torture of Mezentius" mentioned on the previous page) toward practical advice on social selection and the defense of one's intellectual work.</meta>

### OF XÉFOLIUS. 329 ###

And since the influence we exert upon one another affects not only our present happiness, but can also advance or delay our internal transformation <note>original: "modification"—in the philosophical language of the 18th century, this often refers to the gradual development or change of the human spirit or soul toward its ultimate state</note>, pay close attention to your choice of company, as well as the moral atmosphere that circulates in the place where you wish to pray in community, or retire to end your days.

->CLXVI.<-

THE **mother-truth** <note>original: "mère-vérité"—a foundational or generative truth from which all other principles are derived</note> of human nature is that we are compelled to seek a happiness that we can only find in the **harmony** whose principles I have established. By means of this key, as everyone is capable of adding infinitely more articles to mine and explaining the conduct of those held in darkness and led astray by the errors they profess, I close my writing with this advice: If you publish a work, do not respond to the criticisms made of it; because if it is good, it will defend itself, and if it is bad, there is no eloquence, no friends, no **cabals** <note>A "cabale" or cabal refers to a secret group of people united to promote their own interests or protect a specific reputation through intrigue.</note> that can save it from the fate it deserves.

But the greater, newer, and deeper a truth is, the more it will be exposed to the con-

<summary>Xéfolius concludes his manual by advising readers to choose their social and spiritual environments carefully and argues that true happiness is found only in moral harmony. He further advises authors to let their work stand on its own merits rather than engaging with critics.</summary>

<keywords>choice of company, internal transformation, mother-truth, harmony, literary criticism, cabals, human nature</keywords>

# LE MANUEL #

tradiction, and the more time will be required for the small number of wise, meditative men and friends of **humanity**, to overcome the phalanxes that **error** and the **self-interest** of a few sanctioned rogues <note>original: "fourbes accrédités"—referring to deceptive individuals who have gained social standing or authority</note> will oppose to it, and to instill their opinion into the minds of so many people who are incapable—despite their lack of modesty—of having one of their own.

Perhaps it will also not be useless to observe, for the last time, that **ignorance** of the **nature of our being** and of the point of harmony—an ignorance in which the majority of men live—is the reason why in this matter, more than in any other, what is evident to one may appear absurd and ridiculous to another; for the paths of **transformation** <note>original: "modification"—as noted on the previous page, this refers to the internal spiritual development or the "shaping" of the soul</note> are infinite, as are the various degrees of transformation that men have reached.

Nevertheless, each will first recognize both his heart and his **experience** in what I say specifically for him; and if he perseveres in reading me, in **meditating** upon my words, and in observing men and the course of things, he will eventually understand me entirely.

One who might have laughed at my simplicity, coming

<summary>Xéfolius explains that the slow acceptance of profound truths is due to the opposition of self-interested elites and the varying degrees of spiritual development among individuals. He encourages the reader to persevere through meditation and observation to achieve a full understanding of these principles.</summary>

<page-num>331</page-num>

### OF XEFOLIUS ###

at last to suspect that **happiness** could not exist without **moral balance**, just as **health** cannot exist without **physical balance**, and that the disruption <note>original: "l'altération"—referring to a change, corruption, or weakening of a state</note> of either is necessarily nothing other than the result of a deviation from its **harmony**, will come to learn from that which he had initially despised, the very happiness so fruitlessly sought from those things which the common crowd <note>original: "le vulgaire"—meaning the unrefined masses or the general public who prioritize material or superficial pursuits</note> prizes most.

->THE END<-

<summary>Xefolius concludes by asserting that happiness is the result of moral balance and harmony, much like health depends on physical balance. He suggests that true fulfillment is found in the spiritual principles that the masses typically overlook.</summary>

<page-num>330</page-num>

<header>THE MANUAL</header>

[con]tradiction; and the more time will be required for the small number of wise, meditative men and friends of **humanity** to overcome the phalanxes <note>original: "phalanges"—referring here to organized ranks or groups of opponents</note> that **error** and the **personal interest** of a few well-regarded deceivers will set against them, and to instill their opinion into the minds of so many people who are incapable—despite their lack of modesty—of having an opinion of their own.

Perhaps it will also not be useless to observe, for the last time, that **ignorance** of the **nature of our being** and of the <term>harmonious point</term> <note>The "point harmonieux" is a central concept for the author, referring to a state of perfect internal or spiritual equilibrium.</note>—an **ignorance** in which the greater part of mankind lives—is the reason why in this matter, more than in any other, what is evident to one may appear absurd and ridiculous to another. This is because the <term>modifying paths</term> <note>The author uses "modification" to describe the varying states of development or shifts in character and perspective that a person undergoes.</note> are infinite, as are the different degrees of **modification** that men have reached.

However, each person will first recognize both their own heart and their own **experience** in what I say primarily for them; and if they persevere in reading me, in meditating upon my words, and in observing men and the course of things, they will eventually understand me entirely.

He who might have laughed at my simplicity, coming

<summary>The author explains that the progress of truth is hindered by the self-interest of deceivers and the spiritual ignorance of the masses. # ERRATA.

PAGE 9, line 16, or allegories; read or do they constitute
->allegories.<-
11, line 5, delete the word so.
15, line 16, child without a whip; read child without strength. <note>original: "enfant sans fouet; lis. enfant sans force." This correction clarifies that the text refers to a lack of physical power rather than a literal whip.</note>
31, line 16, we might consult; read we should consult. <note>The original correction changes the French verb tense from the present subjunctive "consultions" to the more formal imperfect subjunctive "consultassions."</note>
37, line 8, their victory; read their victories.
108, line 23, the other toward which; read the cave toward which. <note>original: "l'autre" (the other) vs "l'antre" (the cave). This is likely a correction of a common typesetting error where 'u' and 'n' were swapped.</note>
113, 1st line. who will have; read who have.
146, line 17, to surprise; read to suspend.
169, line 16, than those; read than of those.
178, line 2, than to madness; read than madness.
181, line 19, the sorrows; read his sorrows.
183, line 22, innocent; read unknown.
199, 1st line. for one; read by one.
245, line 2, to threaten them; read to threaten him.
268, line 3, naturatives; read <term>maturatives</term>. <note>maturatives: In historical medicine, these were substances applied to the skin to encourage a wound or abscess to "ripen" or heal.</note>
303, 1st line. in not doubting; read not doubting.
305, line 17, you only see one; read you no longer see but one.
319, line 18, the opinions; read & the opinions.
